- Choose a recurring performance problem and compare the likely result of a motivation-only intervention with an intervention based on a wider cause analysis.
Key point:
- A solution must match the barrier.
- More pressure is not a substitute for skill, support, resources, or workable systems.
Source links:
- Source: Chapter 6, “Diagnose What Happened,” Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed. (source text supplied in Box; no external URL supplied or consulted).
4. Q: What are the Six Sources of Influence?
A: Show answer
The model organizes influences on behavior across three domains—personal, social, and structural—and two dimensions—motivation and ability. The six sources are personal motivation, personal ability, social motivation, social ability, structural motivation, and structural ability. Personal sources concern what the individual wants and can do. Social sources concern how other people encourage, discourage, help, or obstruct the behavior. Structural sources concern nonhuman conditions such as rewards, penalties, tools, technology, policies, processes, information, and the physical or virtual environment. The framework is a diagnostic grid, not a claim that every problem has exactly one cause. Its value is that it prompts a systematic search across influences that a blame-focused explanation may overlook.
Study instruction:
- Draw a three-row by two-column grid and place a current work problem in each relevant cell, recording evidence for every proposed influence.
Key point:
- Three domains multiplied by two dimensions produce six diagnostic sources.
- Use the model to broaden inquiry, not to force every case into a single category.
Source links:
- Source: Chapter 6, “Diagnose What Happened,” Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed. (source text supplied in Box; no external URL supplied or consulted).
5. Q: How do motivation and ability differ in the Six Sources of Influence model?
A: Show answer
Motivation concerns whether a person wants to perform the required behavior; ability concerns whether the person can perform it. A person may understand a task and possess the necessary skills but choose not to do it. Another person may be willing and committed but lack the knowledge, confidence, physical capacity, authority, information, or practical support required. These conditions can coexist: a difficult task may reduce willingness, and repeated failure may weaken confidence. The distinction matters because the interventions differ. Motivation barriers may call for clarifying purpose, examining values or incentives, or addressing social pressure. Ability barriers may require training, practice, resources, permissions, assistance, or redesign of the work. A responsible diagnosis tests both rather than assuming one.
Study instruction:
- For a specific gap, write one plausible motivation explanation and one plausible ability explanation, then identify what evidence would distinguish them.
Key point:
- Wanting to do a task and being able to do it are separate questions.
- Check both dimensions because one barrier can conceal or reinforce the other.
Source links:
- Source: Chapter 6, “Diagnose What Happened,” Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed. (source text supplied in Box; no external URL supplied or consulted).
6. Q: How are the personal, social, and structural domains distinguished?
A: Show answer
The personal domain concerns influences located within the individual, including interests, values, knowledge, skills, and physical or cognitive capabilities. The social domain concerns other people: colleagues, supervisors, family members, peers, and anyone whose approval, pressure, help, information, or permission affects the behavior. The structural domain concerns nonhuman conditions, including incentives, rules, job design, equipment, software, access to data, process steps, workspace, distance, and communication infrastructure. These domains are not mutually exclusive. A worker may lack confidence personally, receive pressure from colleagues socially, and face a confusing system structurally. Separating the domains helps the investigator avoid treating a system problem as a personality defect or overlooking the contribution of the surrounding team.
Study instruction:
- Classify the possible causes of a team problem under personal, social, and structural headings and explain why each belongs in that domain.
Key point:
- Personal means the individual; social means other people; structural means nonhuman conditions.
- Multiple domains can contribute to the same observed behavior.
Source links:
- Source: Chapter 6, “Diagnose What Happened,” Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed. (source text supplied in Box; no external URL supplied or consulted).
7. Q: What is personal motivation, and what questions reveal a personal-motivation barrier?
A: Show answer
Personal motivation concerns the intrinsic meaning, interest, satisfaction, discomfort, or value a person associates with doing the behavior. The diagnostic question is whether the person wants to do it when considered apart from external pressure. A task may feel boring, repetitive, unpleasant, embarrassing, exhausting, or inconsistent with the person's values. The individual may prefer an alternative activity that is more rewarding in the moment. Statements such as “I do not like this,” “That is not for me,” or “It does not feel worthwhile” can signal this source, but they should be explored rather than treated as a complete diagnosis. Ask what makes the behavior unattractive, what outcome matters to the person, and whether the task can be made more meaningful or manageable without lowering a legitimate standard.
Study instruction:
- Identify a behavior that someone may find intrinsically unpleasant and prepare open questions about meaning, discomfort, values, and perceived benefit.
Key point:
- Personal motivation is the individual's internal desire or reluctance.
- A motivation barrier should be explored before assuming defiance or indifference.
Source links:
- Source: Chapter 6, “Diagnose What Happened,” Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed. (source text supplied in Box; no external URL supplied or consulted).
8. Q: What is personal ability, and why should it be examined before concluding that someone is unwilling?
A: Show answer
Personal ability asks whether the individual, acting with their own knowledge and skills, can perform the behavior. Relevant capabilities may be technical, cognitive, physical, interpersonal, or emotional. A customer-service employee may avoid difficult calls because they have not learned how to de-escalate hostility. A new team member may work slowly because they have not yet mastered the equipment. An employee may avoid a digital workflow because they are unsure how to use it. These situations can look like resistance from the outside, but the person may be willing and unable. The appropriate response may include instruction, demonstration, supervised practice, coaching, accessible job aids, or a reasonable adjustment. Verify capability in practice rather than relying only on a verbal assurance.
Study instruction:
- Select a task with a possible skill barrier and specify how you would observe or test the person's ability without humiliating them.
Key point:
- Personal ability is about capability, not willingness.
- Training and supported practice are appropriate when a genuine skill gap is present.
Source links:
- Source: Chapter 6, “Diagnose What Happened,” Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed. (source text supplied in Box; no external URL supplied or consulted).
9. Q: Why can motivation and ability influence one another rather than operate independently?
A: Show answer
Motivation and ability frequently reinforce each other. A person who finds a task difficult may become discouraged and less willing to attempt it. Repeated failure can create embarrassment or a belief that effort is pointless. Conversely, someone who sees value in a task may practice more, seek help, and build capability. A highly motivated employee may still be unable to succeed when a task requires unfamiliar skills or inaccessible resources. For this reason, diagnosing only one dimension can leave the other barrier untouched. After identifying a likely skill problem, ask whether the person is willing to perform the task once the barrier is removed. After clarifying the importance of the task, ask whether anything still prevents the person from doing it.
Study instruction:
- Map a two-way relationship between a motivation barrier and an ability barrier in one real example, noting how each may intensify the other.
Key point:
- Motivation and ability can be simultaneous and mutually reinforcing.
- After addressing one, deliberately test whether the other remains.
Source links:
- Source: Chapter 6, “Diagnose What Happened,” Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed. (source text supplied in Box; no external URL supplied or consulted).
10. Q: What is social motivation, and how can peer pressure shape behavior at work?
A: Show answer
Social motivation concerns the way other people's approval, disapproval, expectations, status, and example influence what an individual wants to do. Employees may follow a shortcut because respected colleagues endorse it, remain silent because the group treats questions as disloyal, or ignore a standard because a supervisor signals that speed matters more than compliance. Peer pressure does not need to be explicit. Silence, jokes, informal norms, praise, exclusion, and visible role modeling can all communicate which behavior earns acceptance. A person may privately believe that a procedure is important but abandon it to avoid embarrassment or conflict. Diagnosis should therefore ask whose opinions matter, what behavior is rewarded socially, and whether influential people send conflicting messages.
Study instruction:
- Identify the important reference groups in a workplace scenario and describe how their approval or disapproval could influence the behavior.
Key point:
- Social motivation is pressure or encouragement from people, not an external financial reward.
- Informal norms and silence can influence conduct as strongly as direct instructions.
Source links:
- Source: Chapter 6, “Diagnose What Happened,” Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed. (source text supplied in Box; no external URL supplied or consulted).
11. Q: What is social ability, and how can other people enable or obstruct performance?
A: Show answer
Social ability concerns the practical help, information, materials, cooperation, authority, or permission that other people provide or withhold. A project officer may be accountable for a report but unable to complete it because another department has not supplied the data. A staff member may need a manager's approval before proceeding. A remote employee may depend on colleagues who are unavailable or fail to respond. In these cases, the individual may possess the relevant skills and want to complete the task, yet cannot succeed without other people's contribution. Ask who must provide what, when it is needed, whether responsibilities are clear, and whether the necessary support was actually available. The diagnosis may require changing handoffs, clarifying authority, or securing a service commitment from another person.
Study instruction:
- For a task involving dependencies, create a simple chain showing each person who must provide information, approval, resources, or assistance and when.
Key point:
- Social ability is help or obstruction supplied by other people.
- Accountability should consider dependencies that the accountable person cannot control alone.
Source links:
- Source: Chapter 6, “Diagnose What Happened,” Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed. (source text supplied in Box; no external URL supplied or consulted).
12. Q: Why does the person conducting the accountability conversation need to examine their own contribution?
A: Show answer
The person raising the concern is part of the social environment and may unintentionally contribute to the gap. They may give conflicting instructions, withhold information, delay approvals, fail to provide promised resources, react harshly to questions, or create a climate in which people hide mistakes. A manager who criticizes an employee for missing a deadline may discover that the manager changed priorities several times and did not communicate the latest requirement. A parent or team leader may also forget a dependency that they had promised to provide. Examining one's own role is not an exercise in accepting blame for everything. It is a disciplined search for influence that can be changed by the person seeking improvement.
Study instruction:
- Ask “What did I do or fail to do that may have made this behavior more likely?” and record at least one testable possibility.
Key point:
- The investigator is part of the social system being diagnosed.
- Self-examination can reveal barriers that the other person cannot remove alone.
Source links:
- Source: Chapter 6, “Diagnose What Happened,” Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed. (source text supplied in Box; no external URL supplied or consulted).
13. Q: What is structural motivation, and how can rewards or penalties create a behavioral gap?
A: Show answer
Structural motivation concerns nonhuman incentives and disincentives that make a behavior appear more or less attractive. Pay, bonuses, commissions, promotion criteria, status, recognition, performance ratings, sanctions, and the perceived risk of losing benefits can shape choices. A company may publicly emphasize quality while rewarding only output volume. Staff may then feel pressure to rush work or bypass checks. A manager may be measured on cost reduction while frontline employees are rewarded for overtime, creating competing incentives. The key diagnostic question is whether the formal or informal reward system makes the desired behavior costly and the undesired behavior advantageous. The solution may require aligning measures, recognition, workload expectations, and consequences with the standard being requested.
Study instruction:
- Compare the behavior an organization says it values with the behavior its targets, bonuses, performance measures, and penalties actually reward.
Key point:
- Structural motivation arises from incentives and disincentives built into the environment.
- People may respond rationally to rewards that are misaligned with stated priorities.
Source links:
- Source: Chapter 6, “Diagnose What Happened,” Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed. (source text supplied in Box; no external URL supplied or consulted).
14. Q: What is structural ability, and which nonhuman barriers should be investigated?
A: Show answer
Structural ability concerns whether tools, technology, policies, processes, information systems, physical spaces, schedules, and other nonhuman conditions make the required behavior possible. A team may be willing and trained but unable to complete work because software is unreliable, data access is restricted, approval steps are excessive, equipment is defective, or responsibilities are poorly designed. Distance and time-zone differences can also make coordination difficult. Structural ability is not limited to expensive technology; a missing checklist, confusing form, poorly placed workstation, or unclear workflow can be enough to obstruct performance. The diagnostic question is what in the environment makes success harder than it needs to be, and what change would remove friction while preserving necessary safeguards.
Study instruction:
- Walk through a process from beginning to end and record each tool, approval, information source, handoff, and environmental condition that could block completion.
Key point:
- Structural ability concerns the design of the work environment and its tools.
- A system that makes the right behavior difficult can undermine capable and willing people.
Source links:
- Source: Chapter 6, “Diagnose What Happened,” Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed. (source text supplied in Box; no external URL supplied or consulted).
15. Q: How should a manager distinguish structural motivation from structural ability?
A: Show answer
Structural motivation asks whether rewards, penalties, status, or performance measures make the person want to behave in a particular way. Structural ability asks whether nonhuman conditions make the behavior feasible. If workers skip quality checks because bonuses depend on units produced, the incentive is a structural-motivation issue. If they cannot complete the checks because the inspection device is broken or the system does not allow enough time to record results, the barrier is structural ability. Both may exist together: a defective tool can slow work while an output-only bonus encourages employees to bypass it. Correct classification matters because changing a bonus will not repair equipment, and purchasing equipment will not necessarily correct a reward system that encourages shortcuts.
Study instruction:
- Classify three workplace examples as structural motivation, structural ability, or both, and explain the evidence that supports each classification.
Key point:
- Rewards and consequences belong to structural motivation.
- Tools, processes, access, and environment belong to structural ability.
Source links:
- Source: Chapter 6, “Diagnose What Happened,” Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed. (source text supplied in Box; no external URL supplied or consulted).
16. Q: Why can physical or virtual proximity influence collaboration?
A: Show answer
Proximity affects how easily people encounter one another, exchange information, ask small questions, and build familiarity. Teams located near each other may have more informal opportunities to clarify dependencies or resolve misunderstandings. When departments are physically separated, interaction may require deliberate scheduling and formal communication. Virtual teams face a similar challenge across locations and time zones. Shared collaboration platforms, predictable check-ins, accessible channels, and well-designed team routines can create forms of virtual proximity. Proximity alone does not guarantee trust or cooperation; unresolved conflict and poor incentives may remain. It is a structural-ability lever because the environment can make useful interaction easier or harder.
Study instruction:
- Map the points at which two groups need to coordinate and identify a physical or virtual arrangement that would make those interactions easier.
Key point:
- Proximity can increase opportunities for interaction and coordination.
- Virtual proximity must be deliberately designed rather than assumed.
Source links:
- Source: Chapter 6, “Diagnose What Happened,” Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed. (source text supplied in Box; no external URL supplied or consulted).
17. Q: How can the six diagnostic sources be used as a practical matrix?
A: Show answer
A useful matrix places the three domains—personal, social, and structural—on one axis and motivation and ability on the other. For personal motivation, ask whether the individual values or wants the behavior; for personal ability, ask whether they have the necessary skills and capacity. For social motivation, examine peer pressure and the expectations of influential people; for social ability, examine help, information, authority, and cooperation from others. For structural motivation, inspect rewards, penalties, status, and measures; for structural ability, inspect tools, processes, policies, access, and the work environment. The matrix should guide questions and evidence collection. It is not a checklist that requires inventing a cause in every cell.
Study instruction:
- Complete all six cells for a current performance gap, writing “no evidence identified” where appropriate rather than forcing a speculative explanation.
Key point:
- The matrix prevents premature focus on one source.
- Evidence may support several cells, while some cells may not be relevant.
Source links:
- Source: Chapter 6, “Diagnose What Happened,” Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed. (source text supplied in Box; no external URL supplied or consulted).
18. Q: What does the almond-farm scenario teach about diagnosing a performance problem?
A: Show answer
The scenario concerns damage to almond trees caused during harvesting with tree-shaker machines. The shift director initially interprets the damage as operator carelessness and is ready to confront the workers. Further information reveals that the inflation mechanism on several new machines is faulty, which points to structural ability. The director also recognizes that workers are paid by harvest volume rather than tree care, a structural-motivation factor. Team members are assigned to watch for damage, which creates a social-ability dependency. Team chants that pressure operators to move faster and competition for the largest harvest create social-motivation pressure. Inexperienced temporary workers point to possible personal-ability gaps. The case shows that a visible failure can have multiple interacting causes.
Study instruction:
- Reconstruct the almond-farm scenario and place each fact—faulty equipment, volume pay, monitoring roles, team pressure, competition, and inexperienced operators—into the six-source matrix.
Key point:
- The same outcome can arise from several sources of influence.
- A complete diagnosis is more useful than an immediate accusation against operators.
Source links:
- Source: Chapter 6, “Diagnose What Happened,” Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed. (source text supplied in Box; no external URL supplied or consulted).
19. Q: Why would reprimanding the almond-farm operators alone be an inadequate response?
A: Show answer
A reprimand might communicate that tree damage is unacceptable, but it would not repair faulty protective-grip mechanisms, realign volume-based incentives, clarify monitoring responsibilities, reduce team pressure, or train inexperienced operators. If those conditions remain, the behavior or damage may continue despite the warning. A reprimand could also make employees less willing to report equipment faults or operational pressures. Accountability still matters: operators must follow standards and report problems. However, responsibility should be matched with the authority, training, tools, and incentives needed to meet the standard. The more effective response combines clear expectations with correction of the identified barriers and verification that the revised system works.
Study instruction:
- Design a corrective-action plan for the almond farm that includes equipment repair, incentive review, role clarity, training, and follow-up checks.
Key point:
- Punishment alone does not remove environmental or capability barriers.
- Accountability is stronger when expectations and enabling conditions are addressed together.
Source links:
- Source: Chapter 6, “Diagnose What Happened,” Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed. (source text supplied in Box; no external URL supplied or consulted).
20. Q: Which questions should a person ask before an accountability conversation to broaden the initial story?
A: Show answer
The chapter recommends pausing before the conversation and asking six broad questions: What motivation problems might exist? Could ability be involved? Are other people exerting competing pressure, including the investigator? Who might be helping or hindering the person's ability to complete the work? What incentives or disincentives may be operating? What nonhuman factors—roles, resources, policies, processes, tools, or distance—may be influencing the outcome? These questions are designed to interrupt a quick character judgment and open several plausible lines of inquiry. They do not require the investigator to know the answer in advance. Their purpose is to prepare a curious, evidence-seeking conversation rather than a prosecution.
Study instruction:
- Use the six pre-conversation questions to prepare for one real discussion and note which facts you need to verify with the other person.
Key point:
- Pause and broaden the explanation before speaking.
- The questions prepare inquiry; they do not predetermine the diagnosis.
Source links:
- Source: Chapter 6, “Diagnose What Happened,” Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed. (source text supplied in Box; no external URL supplied or consulted).
21. Q: How should diagnostic questions move from broad exploration to specific causes?
A: Show answer
The chapter recommends a funnel. Begin with a broad, genuinely curious question about what happened and invite the person to describe their experience. Listen for indications of motivation or ability barriers, then narrow the inquiry toward personal, social, or structural influences. For example, after learning that a report was late, ask what got in the way. If the person mentions waiting for data, explore who controls the data, whether the request was clear, and whether access or timing was realistic. If the person mentions uncertainty, explore training and confidence. Narrowing too quickly can make the conversation feel like interrogation or lead the investigator to impose a theory. A broad opening gives the other person room to supply information that the investigator may not know.
Study instruction:
- Write a five-question funnel for a missed commitment: one open question, two questions about motivation and ability, and two questions about a likely source of influence.
Key point:
- Start with “What happened?” in a genuinely curious manner.
- Move from broad explanation to the specific source supported by the answer.
Source links:
- Source: Chapter 6, “Diagnose What Happened,” Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed. (source text supplied in Box; no external URL supplied or consulted).
22. Q: Why is diagnosis during a live conversation difficult?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies several challenges. First, behavior is complex and multiple influences may operate at once: a person may dislike a task, lack experience, and depend on someone who does not provide required information. Second, explanations may be vague, incomplete, or disingenuous. A person may say that “things came up” without identifying the obstacle, or may feel embarrassed to admit a skill gap or lack of interest. Third, diagnosis can sound like excuse-making, as though the investigator is abandoning the commitment while discussing barriers. These difficulties require patience, precise follow-up questions, and a clear distinction between understanding causes and deciding what responsibility remains. The investigator should avoid both hostile cross-examination and uncritical acceptance of every explanation.
Study instruction:
- Prepare follow-up questions for a vague explanation such as “I was too busy” that clarify competing priorities, capacity, support, and decisions made.
Key point:
- Complex causes, unclear explanations, and fear of excusing behavior complicate live diagnosis.
- Stay curious while keeping the original expectation visible.
Source links:
- Source: Chapter 6, “Diagnose What Happened,” Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed. (source text supplied in Box; no external URL supplied or consulted).
23. Q: How can an investigator respond when an explanation is ambiguous or vague?
A: Show answer
A vague explanation should be explored respectfully rather than accepted as a complete account or treated immediately as dishonesty. If someone says “something came up,” ask what specifically changed, when it happened, what options were considered, and when the risk to the commitment became apparent. If the person says they lacked support, ask what help was needed, from whom, and whether it was requested. The aim is to turn general statements into observable events, dependencies, and decisions. Avoid leading questions that suggest the answer you want. If facts remain uncertain, record the uncertainty and identify what can be verified. A clear account helps both parties distinguish genuine barriers from avoidable choices.
Study instruction:
- Convert three vague explanations into neutral, non-leading follow-up questions that seek dates, actions, dependencies, and evidence.
Key point:
- Vague statements need clarification, not automatic acceptance or accusation.
- Specific facts make it possible to identify the real barrier and responsibility.
Source links:
- Source: Chapter 6, “Diagnose What Happened,” Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed. (source text supplied in Box; no external URL supplied or consulted).
24. Q: How can a person diagnose barriers without appearing to excuse a missed commitment?
A: Show answer
The investigator should explicitly hold two ideas together: the commitment remains important, and understanding what prevented completion is necessary to solve the problem. A useful explanation is that the purpose of exploring barriers is to determine what must change so the expected result can be achieved reliably. The person can acknowledge the impact of the missed commitment while asking about skill, support, competing demands, incentives, and process constraints. Once the barrier is understood, the conversation should return to responsibility: what will the individual do, what support will others provide, and what standard applies next time? Diagnosis becomes excuse-making only if it removes expectations without a reasoned decision or agreed corrective action.
Study instruction:
- Draft a sentence that acknowledges the impact of a missed commitment while explaining that the discussion will examine barriers and preserve a clear future expectation.
Key point:
- Understanding a cause does not automatically remove responsibility.
- Link diagnosis to a specific plan for meeting the standard in future.
Source links:
- Source: Chapter 6, “Diagnose What Happened,” Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed. (source text supplied in Box; no external URL supplied or consulted).
25. Q: Why should diagnostic conversations check both motivation and ability even after one appears to explain the gap?
A: Show answer
The chapter warns that most gaps should not be assumed to involve only motivation or only ability. A person may lack a skill and also be reluctant to perform the task. Alternatively, a person may understand why the task matters but still face an obstacle that makes success impossible. If the investigator stops after finding one barrier, a second barrier may continue to block performance. After discussing ability, ask whether the person is willing to perform the behavior once the obstacle is removed. After clarifying motivation, ask whether anything still prevents them from being able to do it. These paired questions help distinguish willingness from capability and avoid designing an incomplete solution.
Study instruction:
- For one case, write the two diagnostic checks: whether the person is willing if barriers are removed, and whether they are able if motivation is present.
Key point:
- Finding one cause does not prove that the other dimension is clear.
- Test willingness and capability separately before finalizing the plan.
Source links:
- Source: Chapter 6, “Diagnose What Happened,” Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed. (source text supplied in Box; no external URL supplied or consulted).
26. Q: How should a solution be matched to the source of influence identified?
A: Show answer
The solution should change the condition that is actually producing or sustaining the behavior. A personal-ability barrier may require training, practice, coaching, or a job aid. A personal-motivation barrier may require exploring meaning, values, discomfort, or the perceived benefit of the task. Social-motivation barriers may require addressing peer norms, conflicting instructions, or the behavior of influential colleagues. Social-ability barriers may require securing information, approvals, assistance, or clearer handoffs. Structural-motivation barriers may require aligning incentives and performance measures. Structural-ability barriers may require repairing tools, simplifying a process, changing access, or redesigning the environment. Some cases need a coordinated package rather than one intervention.
Study instruction:
- For each of the six sources, identify one practical intervention and one observable sign that the intervention has removed the relevant barrier.
Key point:
- Interventions should be cause-specific.
- A single generic warning or training session is unlikely to resolve every type of barrier.
Source links:
- Source: Chapter 6, “Diagnose What Happened,” Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed. (source text supplied in Box; no external URL supplied or consulted).
27. Q: How can a manager prioritize several causes when multiple sources contribute to the same gap?
A: Show answer
Start by distinguishing immediate safety or compliance risks from performance improvements that can be planned over time. Then identify which causes are strongly supported by evidence, which are within the manager's control, and which require cooperation from others. Address critical structural failures—such as defective equipment or inaccessible information—before expecting individuals to compensate indefinitely. Clarify non-negotiable standards and assign owners to dependencies. Where several barriers interact, sequence the interventions so one change does not undermine another. For example, training may be ineffective until a stable tool is available, while incentive changes may be needed at the same time to prevent pressure to bypass the new procedure. Prioritization should be transparent and revisited as new evidence emerges.
Study instruction:
- Rank the causes in a multi-factor case by urgency, evidence, controllability, and dependency, then justify the order of corrective actions.
Key point:
- Prioritize immediate risks and evidence-supported causes.
- Coordinate interventions when barriers depend on one another.
Source links:
- Source: Chapter 6, “Diagnose What Happened,” Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed. (source text supplied in Box; no external URL supplied or consulted).
28. Q: How can a manager test whether a proposed diagnosis is accurate rather than merely plausible?
A: Show answer
A diagnosis becomes stronger when it generates predictions that can be checked. If the suspected barrier is lack of skill, observe the task, review work samples, or provide a practice opportunity. If missing information is the cause, verify when the information was requested and delivered. If incentives are driving shortcuts, compare formal targets with actual behavior and decisions. If peer pressure is involved, examine instructions, team norms, and examples of how colleagues respond. Ask the person whether the proposed explanation fits their experience and invite correction. Keep alternative explanations open until evidence supports one more strongly. This approach reduces the risk of replacing one untested story with another.
Study instruction:
- For one suspected barrier, state what evidence would support it, what evidence would weaken it, and how you could obtain both fairly.
Key point:
- A plausible explanation is not yet a verified cause.
- Use observation, records, questions, and small tests to check the diagnosis.
Source links:
- Source: Chapter 6, “Diagnose What Happened,” Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed. (source text supplied in Box; no external URL supplied or consulted).
29. Q: How can the Six Sources of Influence be applied to employees who avoid a new customer relationship management system?
A: Show answer
Begin by asking what happens when the employee is expected to use the system. Personal motivation may be involved if the employee sees no value or finds the workflow unpleasant. Personal ability may be involved if the employee has not learned the functions or lacks confidence. Social motivation may be involved if respected colleagues discourage adoption or managers send mixed signals. Social ability may be involved if supervisors do not provide coaching or necessary customer data. Structural motivation may be involved if performance measures reward speed in a way that makes system entry seem costly. Structural ability may be involved if the software is unreliable, access is restricted, or the process requires duplicate entry. The diagnosis should identify the actual combination before prescribing a solution.
Study instruction:
- Complete a six-source analysis of a technology-adoption problem and propose one evidence-based intervention for each source that is present.
Key point:
- Technology resistance may reflect skill, social, incentive, or system barriers—not just attitude.
- Verify usability and workflow design alongside employee willingness.
Source links:
- Source: Chapter 6, “Diagnose What Happened,” Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed. (source text supplied in Box; no external URL supplied or consulted).
30. Q: How does the release-testing example illustrate social motivation and conflicting authority?
A: Show answer
In the chapter's release-testing example, a manager urges a tester to hurry because the team is behind schedule and suggests simplifying tests because the software appears to work. The tester may know the formal testing protocol, but a direct request from a manager creates social pressure to skip it. The employee may also receive conflicting signals: official standards require complete testing, while a powerful person appears to reward speed. The behavior is therefore not explained solely by the tester's personal motivation or skill. A diagnosis should examine who gives instructions, how priorities are communicated, whether employees can challenge unsafe shortcuts, and whether managers are held to the same standards. Corrective action may need to address leadership behavior and escalation routes, not just retrain testers.
Study instruction:
- Map the competing messages in the release-testing example and identify how a tester could raise the conflict before a protocol is bypassed.
Key point:
- A manager's informal request can create powerful social pressure.
- Formal standards lose influence when leaders reward or request shortcuts.
Source links:
- Source: Chapter 6, “Diagnose What Happened,” Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed. (source text supplied in Box; no external URL supplied or consulted).
31. Q: How can a team identify whether performance pressure is social or structural?
A: Show answer
Social pressure comes from people and group expectations: a colleague's chant, a supervisor's comments, ridicule for working carefully, or competition for peer approval. Structural pressure comes from nonhuman incentives and systems: output-based pay, targets, performance ratings, or penalties that reward speed over quality. In practice the two can reinforce one another. A volume-based bonus may establish the incentive, while team members amplify it through jokes and competition. Distinguishing them helps identify who must be involved in the solution. A manager may need to change the measurement system and also reset team norms. Simply telling workers to ignore pressure is unlikely to work if the surrounding environment continues to reward the opposite behavior.
Study instruction:
- Analyze a high-pressure team and separate interpersonal messages from formal incentives, then show how they interact.
Key point:
- Social motivation is transmitted by people; structural motivation is built into rewards and consequences.
- The two sources can reinforce the same shortcut or undesirable behavior.
Source links:
- Source: Chapter 6, “Diagnose What Happened,” Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed. (source text supplied in Box; no external URL supplied or consulted).
32. Q: What can be learned from the chapter's discussion of temporary or inexperienced workers?
A: Show answer
A workforce that includes temporary or newly assigned people may contain individuals who have never performed the task before. Placing someone on equipment and saying “you will be fine” does not establish that the person has the required capability. The visible outcome may be treated as carelessness even though the person has not received instruction, demonstration, supervised practice, or feedback. A sound diagnosis checks prior experience, task complexity, safety-critical steps, and the quality of onboarding. It also asks whether the organization has designed staffing and training arrangements that make competent performance realistic. Individual responsibility remains, but managers must not assume competence merely because a person has been assigned a role.
Study instruction:
- Create a minimum competence and onboarding checklist for a task performed by temporary or newly transferred workers.
Key point:
- Assignment to a role does not prove competence.
- Training and verification are especially important when workers are new or temporary.
Source links:
- Source: Chapter 6, “Diagnose What Happened,” Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed. (source text supplied in Box; no external URL supplied or consulted).
33. Q: How can social ability be assessed in a virtual or hybrid team?
A: Show answer
Examine the dependencies that require cooperation across locations: data handoffs, approvals, technical support, shared decisions, and access to colleagues with specialist knowledge. Determine whether people know who to contact, whether response expectations are clear, and whether time-zone differences make support unavailable when needed. A collaboration platform may exist but still fail if channels are fragmented, information is difficult to find, or team members do not respond. Managers should clarify ownership, establish predictable overlap periods, document handoffs, and ensure that employees can obtain help without excessive delay. This is a social-ability diagnosis because other people provide essential assistance, although the digital platform itself may also be a structural factor.
Study instruction:
- Trace one virtual-team task through all human dependencies and identify where an unanswered request or unclear handoff could block completion.
Key point:
- Remote workers can be dependent on others even when they appear to work independently.
- Clear contacts, response norms, and handoffs improve access to social support.
Source links:
- Source: Chapter 6, “Diagnose What Happened,” Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed. (source text supplied in Box; no external URL supplied or consulted).
34. Q: How can an organization distinguish a genuine resource barrier from a convenient excuse?
A: Show answer
The distinction requires specific evidence and a fair examination of the work conditions. Identify exactly which resource was needed, when it was needed, who controlled it, whether it was requested, and what happened after the request. Compare the claimed shortage with inventory, access logs, approval records, workload information, or the experience of others performing the same task. Also ask whether the person used available alternatives or notified the responsible manager when the barrier emerged. A genuine resource problem should be addressed; a person who failed to request available support may still have responsibility for communication and planning. Avoid assuming either bad faith or innocence before the relevant facts are understood.
Study instruction:
- Prepare an evidence checklist for a claimed resource barrier, covering the resource, request, timing, availability, alternatives, and notification.
Key point:
- Test resource claims through facts and process records.
- A real barrier and an avoidable failure to communicate can coexist.
Source links:
- Source: Chapter 6, “Diagnose What Happened,” Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed. (source text supplied in Box; no external URL supplied or consulted).
35. Q: What role should the manager play when the diagnosis reveals that their own instructions contributed to the gap?
A: Show answer
The manager should acknowledge the contribution plainly, correct conflicting or incomplete instructions, and clarify the standard going forward. If priorities changed without notice, the manager should explain which instruction now governs and how future changes will be communicated. If approvals or information were delayed, the manager should establish a reliable response process. Acknowledging one's role does not require abandoning the employee's responsibilities; it makes the allocation of responsibility more accurate. Managers should also ask whether their reactions have discouraged early warnings. When leaders model accountability for their own influence, employees are more likely to discuss barriers before those barriers become larger failures.
Study instruction:
- Draft a concise manager statement that acknowledges a specific contribution to a missed commitment and identifies the corrective change the manager will make.
Key point:
- Managers can create or remove barriers through their own behavior.
- Shared accountability requires each person to own the influence they can change.
Source links:
- Source: Chapter 6, “Diagnose What Happened,” Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed. (source text supplied in Box; no external URL supplied or consulted).
36. Q: How should the diagnosis be translated into a clear accountability agreement?
A: Show answer
After the causes have been explored, return to the required behavior and establish a practical agreement. State what must happen, who owns each action, what support or resource will be provided, when the action is due, and how progress or completion will be verified. Include contingencies for predictable obstacles: whom to notify, how early to raise a risk, and what alternative route is authorized. The agreement should distinguish the individual's commitment from the manager's or colleagues' enabling commitments. Avoid vague promises such as “try harder” or “communicate better.” Use observable actions and realistic deadlines. A written record may be appropriate for complex, recurring, safety-sensitive, or formally managed issues.
Study instruction:
- Convert a diagnosed gap into an action agreement with behavior, owner, support, deadline, evidence, and escalation trigger.
Key point:
- Diagnosis must lead to observable commitments and enabling actions.
- Make each dependency and follow-up responsibility explicit.
Source links:
- Source: Chapter 6, “Diagnose What Happened,” Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed. (source text supplied in Box; no external URL supplied or consulted).
37. Q: How can follow-up determine whether the intervention actually closed the gap?
A: Show answer
Follow-up should examine whether the expected behavior occurred under the conditions that previously produced the problem. Check objective evidence such as completed work, quality results, response times, process records, or observed performance. Ask whether the agreed training, support, approval, or system change was delivered. If performance improves, determine whether the improvement is sustained rather than a one-time response to scrutiny. If the gap remains, revisit the diagnosis: the original cause may have been incomplete, a new barrier may have appeared, or the intervention may not have been implemented as agreed. Follow-up is not merely surveillance; it is the feedback loop that tests whether the problem-solving approach worked.
Study instruction:
- Set a review date and identify two outcome measures and one process measure that will show whether the agreed change is working.
Key point:
- A proposed solution is not proven until its effect is checked.
- Persistent gaps require renewed diagnosis rather than automatic repetition of the same response.
Source links:
- Source: Chapter 6, “Diagnose What Happened,” Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed. (source text supplied in Box; no external URL supplied or consulted).
38. Q: What are the risks of using the Six Sources model as a rigid checklist?
A: Show answer
The model is useful as a prompt for broader thinking, but it should not become a mechanical exercise in which the investigator invents a cause in every cell. Some sources may be irrelevant, and evidence may be stronger for one or two than for the rest. A rigid checklist can also prolong a conversation unnecessarily or make the other person feel interrogated. The investigator should prioritize the sources suggested by the facts, ask questions in a natural sequence, and remain open to unexpected explanations. The framework does not replace professional judgment, formal investigation, safety procedures, or organizational policy. It improves the quality of inquiry when used flexibly and with a clear focus on the actual gap.
Study instruction:
- Review a completed six-source matrix and remove speculative entries that lack evidence, noting which unanswered questions are genuinely important.
Key point:
- The model structures inquiry but does not dictate the answer.
- Use judgment and evidence; do not force a cause into every category.
Source links:
- Source: Chapter 6, “Diagnose What Happened,” Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed. (source text supplied in Box; no external URL supplied or consulted).
39. Q: How should serious misconduct or immediate safety risks be handled when diagnosis is still incomplete?
A: Show answer
An incomplete diagnosis must not delay protective action when there is an immediate threat to people, assets, legal compliance, or critical operations. Follow emergency, safety, safeguarding, incident-reporting, or escalation procedures that apply to the situation. Preserve relevant evidence and avoid actions that could compromise a formal investigation. The Six Sources model can later help explain contributing influences, but it does not replace mandatory controls or determine guilt. For serious allegations, use authorized procedures and ensure that the person raising the concern is protected according to policy. Dialogue is valuable, but the urgency and formality of the response must match the risk.
Study instruction:
- Identify the immediate protective steps and formal reporting route for a high-risk scenario before planning a broader behavioral diagnosis.
Key point:
- Protect people and preserve evidence before attempting informal problem-solving where risk is urgent.
- The influence model complements, but does not replace, formal procedures.
Source links:
- Source: Chapter 6, “Diagnose What Happened,” Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed. (source text supplied in Box; no external URL supplied or consulted).
40. Q: How can leaders use the Six Sources model to improve systems rather than repeatedly correct individuals?
A: Show answer
Leaders can aggregate recurring gaps and look for patterns across teams, roles, and processes. If many employees make the same error, the cause may involve unclear instructions, poor system design, inadequate training, conflicting incentives, or missing support rather than isolated personal failure. Leaders can compare where the behavior occurs, which groups experience it, and what conditions differ between successful and unsuccessful teams. This analysis can guide process redesign, resource allocation, incentive alignment, training, and clearer role definitions. Individual coaching may still be appropriate, but repeated recurrence is a signal to investigate the surrounding system. The objective is to make the desired behavior easier, more understandable, and consistently supported.
Study instruction:
- Choose a recurring organization-wide error and test whether its distribution suggests a common structural or social cause rather than isolated individual shortcomings.
Key point:
- Repeated patterns may indicate system-level causes.
- Sustainable improvement often requires changing the environment as well as individual behavior.
Source links:
- Source: Chapter 6, “Diagnose What Happened,” Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed. (source text supplied in Box; no external URL supplied or consulted).
41. Q: What is the difference between explaining behavior and excusing it?
A: Show answer
Explaining behavior identifies the conditions that contributed to an action; excusing behavior removes or reduces responsibility without a justified basis. A person may miss a deadline because another team failed to provide data, but they may still have been responsible for warning the project manager when the dependency became late. A worker may lack training, while the organization remains responsible for providing it and the worker remains responsible for following safety instructions within their competence. A useful diagnosis allocates responsibility accurately across the people and systems involved. It does not assume that every external barrier absolves the individual, nor that every failure proves bad intent. The standard and the causes must both remain visible.
Study instruction:
- For a missed commitment, identify what the individual controlled, what others controlled, and which responsibilities remain after the causes are understood.
Key point:
- Causal explanation and accountability are compatible.
- Allocate responsibility according to control, authority, commitments, and evidence.
Source links:
- Source: Chapter 6, “Diagnose What Happened,” Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed. (source text supplied in Box; no external URL supplied or consulted).
42. Q: How can the Six Sources model help prevent repeated blame and defensive cycles?
A: Show answer
When people are repeatedly blamed without a careful diagnosis, they may become defensive, conceal problems, or focus on protecting themselves rather than improving performance. The manager may interpret that defensiveness as further proof of bad character, creating a cycle of accusation and withdrawal. A broader inquiry can interrupt the cycle by treating the initial story as provisional, asking about capability and context, and identifying changes both parties can make. The conversation still addresses the gap and its consequences, but it does not reduce the person to the failure. If the evidence ultimately shows unwillingness or repeated disregard for a clear standard, that can be addressed directly and fairly. Curiosity is a method of diagnosis, not avoidance of a conclusion.
Study instruction:
- Map a blame-defensiveness cycle and show where an open diagnostic question and a specific enabling action could interrupt it.
Key point:
- Curiosity can reduce premature judgment and defensiveness.
- A fair diagnosis can still lead to firm accountability when evidence supports it.
Source links:
- Source: Chapter 6, “Diagnose What Happened,” Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed. (source text supplied in Box; no external URL supplied or consulted).
43. Q: How should a leader evaluate whether an incentive system is undermining the behavior the organization expects?
A: Show answer
Compare the stated standard with the measures that affect pay, promotion, recognition, workload, and job security. Ask what employees believe will happen if they slow production to protect quality, report a problem, refuse an unsafe shortcut, or spend time helping a colleague. Examine whether targets reward only volume, speed, or cost reduction while ignoring accuracy, safety, customer outcomes, or cooperation. Look for unintended consequences and differences between formal policy and informal managerial practice. An incentive may not be the only cause, but it can make the desired behavior personally costly. Leaders should align measures and consequences with the real priorities and monitor whether the revised incentives produce the intended behavior without creating new distortions.
Study instruction:
- Audit one performance scorecard by listing its measures, rewards, omissions, and the behaviors each element is likely to encourage.
Key point:
- Employees infer priorities from what the organization measures and rewards.
- Incentive alignment should be tested against actual behavior, not just policy language.
Source links:
- Source: Chapter 6, “Diagnose What Happened,” Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed. (source text supplied in Box; no external URL supplied or consulted).
44. Q: How can a leader assess whether a process or technology is creating a structural-ability barrier?
A: Show answer
Observe the task as employees actually perform it rather than relying only on the documented procedure. Identify duplicate entry, unnecessary approvals, confusing screens, missing data, unreliable equipment, inaccessible instructions, and handoffs that create waiting time. Compare the intended workflow with the real sequence of work, including workarounds employees have developed. Ask users where errors occur and what information or tool would make the task easier. Test proposed changes with representative users and check that they preserve security, quality, and control requirements. A process should not be simplified by removing safeguards without assessing risk. The goal is to remove avoidable friction while retaining necessary controls.
Study instruction:
- Conduct a process walk-through and record the step, obstacle, resulting workaround, risk, and proposed improvement for each structural barrier.
Key point:
- Observe actual work, not only the formal process map.
- Remove unnecessary friction without weakening essential controls.
Source links:
- Source: Chapter 6, “Diagnose What Happened,” Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed. (source text supplied in Box; no external URL supplied or consulted).
45. Q: What advanced judgment is required when a person appears motivated and capable but still fails to perform?
A: Show answer
If the individual appears willing and skilled, expand the inquiry to social and structural conditions and verify whether the behavior is genuinely under the person's control. The person may lack authority, information, timely cooperation, functioning tools, or a realistic opportunity to perform. Conflicting priorities may make the commitment impossible within available time. There may also be an unrecognized decision not to act, which should be explored with evidence rather than assumed away. Ask what happened at the point of action, what the person did when an obstacle appeared, and whether they communicated the risk. This analysis distinguishes inability caused by context from avoidable choices while preserving a fair standard of responsibility.
Study instruction:
- Analyze a case in which training and willingness are established, then test social dependencies, authority, incentives, and structural conditions before reaching a conclusion.
Key point:
- When personal motivation and ability seem adequate, inspect the wider system.
- Do not confuse an unexplained gap with proof of intentional noncompliance.
Source links:
- Source: Chapter 6, “Diagnose What Happened,” Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed. (source text supplied in Box; no external URL supplied or consulted).
46. Q: How can the Six Sources framework be integrated into a repeatable management practice?
A: Show answer
A repeatable practice begins when a gap is observed: pause before assigning motive, state the expected and actual behavior, and ask an open question about what happened. Explore motivation and ability, then examine personal, social, and structural influences suggested by the evidence. Consider the investigator's own contribution and distinguish explanation from excuse. Agree on interventions matched to the causes, clarify each person's responsibility and support commitments, and define how success will be verified. Record recurring patterns so leaders can identify system-level barriers. The framework should be used proportionately: a minor one-off issue may need only a brief conversation, while a complex or high-risk problem may require documentation, specialist input, or formal procedures.
Study instruction:
- Create a one-page management guide covering observation, open inquiry, six-source diagnosis, responsibility, matched intervention, and follow-up.
Key point:
- A consistent sequence turns the framework into a practical management routine.
- Scale the depth of diagnosis to the complexity and risk of the gap.
Source links:
- Source: Chapter 6, “Diagnose What Happened,” Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed. (source text supplied in Box; no external URL supplied or consulted).
47. Q: What are the central lessons of Chapter 6, “Diagnose What Happened”?
A: Show answer
The chapter teaches that effective accountability depends on understanding why a behavioral gap occurred before selecting a response. A quick character-based story is often incomplete because behavior can be shaped by motivation and ability across personal, social, and structural domains. The Six Sources of Influence provides a disciplined way to consider intrinsic motivation, individual capability, peer pressure, help from others, rewards and penalties, and the design of tools and environments. Diagnosis should begin with curiosity, move from broad questions to specific evidence, examine the investigator's own contribution, and check both motivation and ability. Understanding barriers does not eliminate accountability; it allows responsibility and corrective action to be assigned more accurately. Thoughtful diagnosis precedes effective problem-solving.
Study instruction:
- Teach the six sources to a colleague using one workplace example, then demonstrate how the diagnosis changes the corrective action compared with a motivation-only explanation.
Key point:
- Do not conclude before understanding the influences behind the behavior.
- Use all six lenses, verify the causes, match interventions, and follow through.
Source links:
- Source: Chapter 6, “Diagnose What Happened,” Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed. (source text supplied in Box; no external URL supplied or consulted).
Rows: 47
Table 7
Making It Easy: Removing Ability Barriers in Accountability
1. Q: What is the central purpose of the “Make It Easy” skill in accountability conversations?
A: Show answer
“Make It Easy” means identifying and reducing the ability barriers that prevent a person from meeting an expectation. Once the gap has been described and its causes explored, the next task is to help create conditions in which the person can both perform the required behavior and remain motivated to do so. The chapter argues that leaders should not immediately treat a missed commitment as unwillingness. They should first examine whether the work is difficult because the person lacks information, skill, cooperation, resources, workable processes, or other forms of support. The goal is not to excuse a missed expectation; it is to remove avoidable friction so that future performance becomes feasible and sustainable.
Study instruction:
- "Study the chapter’s distinction between identifying a performance gap and diagnosing what prevents the person from closing it. Be able to explain why reducing friction is part of accountability rather than an alternative to it."
Key point:
- Make it easy by removing genuine ability barriers, not by abandoning the expectation.
- Sustainable accountability requires both ability and motivation.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
2. Q: Why does the chapter recommend examining ability before trying to increase motivation?
A: Show answer
Ability barriers are often less visible than motivation problems, so people may overlook them and assume that a person simply does not care. The chapter recommends beginning with ability because exhorting someone to try harder cannot solve a lack of knowledge, skill, resources, cooperation, or access. If a person is unable to perform and receives only pressure to perform, the result may be discouragement rather than improvement. Starting with ability helps reveal practical obstacles that can be removed. It does not mean ability is more important than motivation or that motivation can be ignored. It is a deliberate diagnostic sequence: make the task feasible first, then verify that the person is willing to do it.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the likely result of motivational pressure with the likely result of removing a specific skill, resource, or coordination barrier. Explain why the sequence matters."
Key point:
- Ability is examined first because its barriers are easy to miss.
- After ability is addressed, motivation still needs to be checked.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
3. Q: What does “friction” mean in the context of changing behavior?
A: Show answer
Friction is anything that makes the desired behavior harder to perform than it needs to be. It can arise from a person’s lack of knowledge or skill, from other people’s actions or omissions, or from the surrounding systems and environment. For example, an employee may understand the task but be blocked because a colleague has not supplied required data. Another employee may have willing coworkers but lack access to the correct software or equipment. Friction is not limited to dramatic obstacles; unclear instructions, cumbersome procedures, poorly arranged work, and missing reminders can all make the right action unnecessarily difficult. The practical task is to identify which barriers are actually operating and reduce them.
Study instruction:
- "Take a recurring task and list the personal, social, and structural factors that make the required behavior harder. Distinguish a genuine barrier from a mere preference."
Key point:
- Friction is avoidable difficulty that obstructs the desired behavior.
- Look beyond the individual to relationships, systems, tools, and the work environment.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
4. Q: How does an ability problem differ from a motivation problem?
A: Show answer
An ability problem concerns whether the person can perform the expected behavior under the existing conditions. The person may lack knowledge, skill, confidence, information, cooperation, resources, or a workable process. A motivation problem concerns whether the person wants to perform the behavior or is willing to make the required effort. The same missed commitment can involve either, or both. Someone may be willing to complete a report but unable to interpret a data set without a specialist. Another person may have the necessary expertise and tools but choose to prioritize different work. The distinction cannot safely be made from the missed result alone; it requires respectful inquiry into what happened and what would make future performance possible.
Study instruction:
- "For several missed commitments, write one plausible ability explanation and one plausible motivation explanation. Identify what evidence would distinguish them."
Key point:
- Ability asks whether the person can; motivation asks whether the person is willing.
- A performance gap by itself does not establish the cause.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
5. Q: Why can trying to motivate someone who lacks ability make the situation worse?
A: Show answer
Motivational pressure does not supply missing skills, information, cooperation, or resources. If the person is already trying but cannot overcome a barrier, repeated demands to work harder can communicate that the leader is not listening or does not understand the obstacle. The chapter warns that this approach can create depression rather than change. It may also encourage concealment: the person may stop disclosing difficulties and instead make unrealistic promises to avoid criticism. A more useful response is to identify the specific barrier, involve the person in developing a remedy, and agree on a realistic path forward. Motivation becomes a meaningful next question after the person has a credible opportunity to succeed.
Study instruction:
- "Use the Akira position-paper example to explain why pressure would not replace the unavailable statistical analyst’s expertise. Identify the practical support needed."
Key point:
- Pressure cannot substitute for missing capability or conditions.
- Diagnose and remove the barrier before demanding greater effort.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
6. Q: What does the chapter teach through Joseph’s driver-safety-class story?
A: Show answer
Joseph initially interprets the instructor’s slow progress and apparent disregard for the scheduled end time as a motivation problem: he assumes she is deliberately keeping the class late. When he asks what end time she understands, he discovers that she was told the session lasted three hours. The conflict is resolved when the missing information is surfaced. The story demonstrates how quickly people can form a negative explanation for another person’s behavior and how an apparently intentional failure may actually reflect an information gap. A focused question can test the story before blame escalates. The lesson is to look for an ability explanation, including missing information, before concluding that someone is unwilling.
Study instruction:
- "Retell the sequence from assumption to inquiry to discovery. Practice identifying a neutral question that could test an assumption without accusing the other person."
Key point:
- A plausible negative story is not proof of bad intent.
- A simple information check can reveal and remove an ability barrier.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
- Crucial Learning: Crucial Accountability Reader Resources
7. Q: What does the Akira position-paper scenario illustrate about a leader’s responsibility?
A: Show answer
Akira agreed to deliver a position paper by noon but completed only about 70 percent because the statistical analyst whose help he needed was hospitalized. He had tried to notify his manager, leaving a message when the manager was unavailable. The chapter presents this as an ability barrier rather than a lack of effort. Once the leader understands the situation, the leader’s responsibility is to help remove the obstacle—for example, by finding another qualified source of statistical interpretation or adjusting the work arrangement. Accountability still matters: the paper remains important and the commitment must be addressed. But a useful leader does not simply repeat the deadline or shame the employee for a dependency outside his control.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the commitment, the blocked dependency, the employee’s attempted communication, and the manager’s next practical action. Consider what evidence would help locate an alternative resource."
Key point:
- A missed deadline can result from an external dependency rather than unwillingness.
- Leaders help remove barriers while keeping the required result visible.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
8. Q: Why does the chapter reject coercion as the default leadership response?
A: Show answer
The chapter challenges the idea that leadership is demonstrated by making people produce results regardless of the cost. Guilt, shame, threats, and pressure may generate short-term compliance, but they do not necessarily solve the conditions that made the task difficult. They can also waste effort, damage trust, and discourage people from being candid about obstacles. The authors do not claim that effort or persistence is never required; demanding situations sometimes require people to dig deep. Their point is that leaders should first look for ways to ease unnecessary difficulty, simplify complexity, and remove avoidable burdens. Effective accountability combines clear expectations with practical support rather than treating suffering as evidence of commitment.
Study instruction:
- "Distinguish a legitimate high-effort demand from avoidable friction. Explain why coercion may produce compliance without building the person’s ability to succeed next time."
Key point:
- There is no inherent leadership virtue in making a task harder than necessary.
- Support and problem-solving should precede coercive pressure.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
9. Q: What two practical skills does the chapter recommend for making a task easier?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies two core skills. First, start by asking the other person for ideas about what needs to be done. This makes the conversation a joint problem-solving effort and helps reveal knowledge or solutions the leader may not possess. Second, brainstorm all sources of ability barriers rather than looking only at the individual. The chapter groups ability barriers into personal, social, and structural sources. Using both skills prevents a leader from jumping prematurely to a solution or overlooking the people and systems that shape performance. Together, they help the person participate in diagnosing the problem and help the leader identify a remedy that is workable in the real environment.
Study instruction:
- "Memorize the two skills and practise applying them to a missed commitment. Check that your diagnosis includes the person, relevant others, and the surrounding system."
Key point:
- Ask for ideas before supplying your own answer.
- Systematically examine personal, social, and structural ability barriers.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
10. Q: Why should a leader begin an ability discussion by asking the other person for ideas?
A: Show answer
The person closest to the work may know which step is failing, what has already been tried, or what resource would make the task possible. They may even know the solution but lack permission, materials, or access to implement it. Asking first reveals this knowledge and establishes a partnership rather than a rescue operation. It also helps preserve the other person’s ownership of the problem. A leader who immediately prescribes a fix may miss important facts or unintentionally signal that the employee is not expected to think independently. The chapter therefore recommends inviting the person into the reasoning process before offering a solution.
Study instruction:
- "Practise opening with a question such as, “You’ve been working on the problem. What do you think needs to be done?” Listen fully before suggesting a remedy."
Key point:
- The person doing the work may hold essential diagnostic information.
- Joint problem-solving supports ownership and practical fit.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
11. Q: How should a leader invite ideas without making the other person feel blamed?
A: Show answer
The invitation should communicate that both people are trying to understand and solve the barrier, not identify a culprit. Use a calm, curious tone and ask what would make the work easier or what has prevented progress. Avoid questions that imply incompetence, such as why the person could not handle a supposedly simple task. Make room for the person to describe uncertainty, missing resources, or dependencies without immediately defending the current process. The leader should listen for facts and possibilities, clarify what has already been attempted, and acknowledge useful information. A respectful inquiry makes it more likely that hidden obstacles will be discussed before they become repeated failures.
Study instruction:
- "Rewrite accusatory questions into neutral diagnostic questions. Practise listening without interrupting or immediately explaining why the barrier should not exist."
Key point:
- The tone should signal partnership and curiosity, not fault-finding.
- A safe discussion makes hidden ability barriers easier to disclose.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
12. Q: What does the formula “Effectiveness = accuracy × commitment” mean?
A: Show answer
The chapter explains that a solution must be both sound and genuinely implemented. Accuracy refers to whether the chosen tactic is technically appropriate for solving the problem. Commitment refers to whether the people who must carry it out believe in and support it. The multiplication metaphor emphasizes that weakness in either factor can undermine the overall result. A technically elegant solution that employees resist may be less effective in practice than a somewhat less sophisticated solution they understand and are committed to using. Conversely, enthusiasm cannot make a fundamentally unsuitable solution accurate. Leaders should therefore seek good ideas while involving implementers in the reasoning that creates commitment.
Study instruction:
- "Apply the formula to two alternative solutions: one highly accurate but resisted, and one workable with strong ownership. Explain how each factor affects implementation."
Key point:
- A good solution needs both sound design and implementation commitment.
- Participation can improve commitment without replacing technical judgment.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
13. Q: What is meant by the accuracy of a solution?
A: Show answer
Accuracy is the degree to which the proposed action actually addresses the cause of the performance gap. A solution is accurate when it fits the facts, dependencies, skills, and constraints involved. For example, if a report is blocked because a specialist is unavailable, telling the employee to organize their time better does not address the cause. Finding a qualified substitute or changing the sequence of work may be more accurate. Accuracy requires diagnosis before prescription. Leaders should test whether a proposed fix removes the actual barrier, whether it creates new risks, and whether it is feasible within the relevant policies and responsibilities.
Study instruction:
- "For a real work problem, state the underlying barrier in one sentence and evaluate whether the proposed remedy directly removes it."
Key point:
- Accuracy depends on matching the remedy to the real cause.
- A plausible-sounding solution can still miss the operative barrier.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
14. Q: What is meant by commitment in the chapter’s effectiveness formula?
A: Show answer
Commitment is the willingness of the people who must implement a solution to support and act on it. It is strengthened when people understand the problem, contribute ideas, and see why the chosen approach makes sense. Commitment is not obtained by pretending to ask for input while secretly steering the person toward a predetermined answer. Nor is it the same as passive agreement in a meeting. A leader should check whether the person believes the proposed action is workable and is willing to carry it out. If the person has concerns, those concerns may reveal either an unresolved ability barrier or a motivation issue that needs further discussion.
Study instruction:
