- "After agreeing on a solution, ask the implementer to explain what they will do and whether they are willing and able to do it. Listen for reservations rather than assuming silence means commitment."
Key point:
- Commitment concerns genuine willingness to implement the solution.
- Agreement should be tested through clear ownership and follow-through.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
15. Q: How does involving the other person in problem-solving strengthen their sense of responsibility?
A: Show answer
When leaders repeatedly leap in with answers, employees can learn to wait for the leader to solve each new problem. This transfers responsibility upward and can weaken the employee’s confidence in their own ability to think through challenges. Involving the person reverses that pattern. They contribute to identifying causes, evaluating options, and choosing a workable response. The leader still provides direction, expertise, resources, and boundaries where needed, but does not unnecessarily take over the thinking. Over time, this approach can strengthen the person’s sense of efficacy—their confidence that they can solve problems rather than merely receive instructions.
Study instruction:
- "Review a recurring problem you tend to solve for others. Identify which part of the diagnosis or solution the other person could own next time."
Key point:
- Taking over every solution can create dependence.
- Participation builds ownership and confidence in problem-solving.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
16. Q: Why is asking for ideas not a manipulation technique?
A: Show answer
The chapter explicitly distinguishes genuine involvement from a tactic designed to make people believe that the leader’s idea was their own. The purpose is not to disguise authority or manufacture false ownership. It is to recognize that others have relevant information and ideas, bring those ideas into the open, and let everyone reason through the problem together. A leader may still contribute a preferred solution, but should be transparent about it and remain open to alternatives. Genuine participation allows the group to understand why an action is needed. Manipulative participation merely stages a consultation while the decision has already been made.
Study instruction:
- "Think of a time someone asked for input but appeared to have already decided. Identify the behaviors that made the consultation feel genuine or staged."
Key point:
- Involvement must be authentic, not a disguised persuasion trick.
- Be transparent about your own view while remaining open to other ideas.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
17. Q: What does it mean to bias the other person’s response when asking for ideas?
A: Show answer
A leader biases the response by presenting their own preferred solution before inviting the other person’s thoughts. Because the leader has authority, the suggestion can fill the other person’s attention and make disagreement feel risky. The employee may simply repeat the leader’s idea, withhold a different option, or focus on pleasing the person in power. The chapter recommends asking for the other person’s ideas first, before introducing the leader’s preferred answer. This sequence gives the person a better chance to think independently and helps the leader hear information that might otherwise remain hidden.
Study instruction:
- "Practise asking an open question and waiting for a complete answer before describing your own preferred solution. Notice how the order changes the range of ideas."
Key point:
- Offering your solution first can narrow the other person’s thinking.
- Invite independent ideas before influencing the discussion with your own preference.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
18. Q: Why should a leader avoid pretending to involve others?
A: Show answer
Pretend involvement occurs when a leader asks for ideas but actually intends to steer the person toward a decision already made. The leader may feel obligated to consult or may prefer to avoid openly stating a view. The result is a guessing game in which the employee tries to read the leader’s mind. This can feel manipulative and erode trust. If the leader has a firm constraint or decision, they should explain it honestly and invite input on the parts that remain open—for example, how to implement the requirement or remove barriers. Authenticity means being clear about what can be influenced and what cannot.
Study instruction:
- "Separate a decision’s non-negotiable requirements from its flexible implementation choices. Practise explaining both before asking for input."
Key point:
- Do not present a predetermined outcome as an open consultation.
- Be explicit about which aspects are fixed and where ideas can shape the solution.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
19. Q: Why is it acceptable for a leader to admit that they do not have all the answers?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that confident leaders need not pretend to be omniscient. Saying “I don’t know” or asking whether someone else has an answer can make room for expertise and ideas that the leader lacks. New leaders may fear that asking for input will make them appear incompetent, but refusing to ask can prevent the group from finding the best solution. A leader remains responsible for the decision, standards, and appropriate safeguards; admitting uncertainty does not mean abandoning authority. It demonstrates that problem-solving is a shared intellectual task and that learning is more important than protecting an image of perfect knowledge.
Study instruction:
- "Identify one area where a colleague has more technical knowledge than you. Prepare a direct question that invites their expertise while preserving your responsibility for the final decision."
Key point:
- Leadership does not require pretending to know everything.
- Admitting uncertainty can unlock expertise and better solutions.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
20. Q: What are the three categories of ability barriers in the chapter’s model?
A: Show answer
The chapter groups ability barriers into three categories: personal, social, and structural. Personal ability concerns the individual’s knowledge, skills, or comfort with performing the task. Social ability concerns whether other people help, cooperate, coordinate, or otherwise enable the behavior. Structural ability concerns the physical and organizational environment, including tools, systems, work layout, policies, procedures, and available resources. The categories broaden diagnosis beyond the individual. A person may be capable in isolation but still be blocked by a coworker’s missing contribution or a cumbersome system. Effective problem-solving examines all three rather than assuming the problem resides solely in the employee.
Study instruction:
- "Draw a three-part diagnostic map for a current performance gap. Place each possible barrier under personal, social, or structural ability and identify evidence for each."
Key point:
- Ability barriers may be personal, social, or structural.
- The model requires attention to the person, other people, and the environment.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
21. Q: How do the six sources in the motivation-and-ability grid relate to the chapter’s discussion?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses a three-by-two grid that crosses personal, social, and structural influences with motivation and ability. The ability sources are Source 2, personal ability; Source 4, social ability; and Source 6, structural ability. The corresponding odd-numbered sources concern motivation. This chapter focuses on the three ability sources because the task is to remove friction. The grid helps a leader avoid treating a performance gap as a single-cause problem. It also reminds the leader to return to motivation after ability barriers have been examined, rather than assuming that resolving one side of the model completes the accountability conversation.
Study instruction:
- "Recreate the six-cell grid from memory. Label Sources 2, 4, and 6 as ability and explain how the personal, social, and structural rows differ."
Key point:
- Ability is represented by Sources 2, 4, and 6.
- The full model includes motivation as well as ability; this chapter concentrates on the ability side.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
22. Q: Why must leaders brainstorm all possible sources of ability barriers?
A: Show answer
The chapter contrasts the way motivation and ability operate in the influence model. A single strong source of motivation may be enough to overcome other motivational detractors. Ability is different: one significant barrier can prevent performance even when other enabling conditions are present. A person may know what to do and have the right equipment, yet still be blocked because a coworker has not completed a required step. If the leader examines only personal skill or only resources, the unresolved barrier can continue to defeat the plan. Brainstorming all three categories makes the diagnosis more complete and reduces the risk of solving the wrong problem.
Study instruction:
- "For one task, identify at least one possible barrier in each ability category. Ask which single unresolved barrier could still prevent completion even if the others were fixed."
Key point:
- One unresolved ability barrier can be enough to block performance.
- Do not stop diagnosing after finding the first plausible obstacle.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
23. Q: What is a personal ability barrier, or Source 2?
A: Show answer
A personal ability barrier exists when the individual lacks something needed to perform the behavior. This may involve knowledge, technical skill, practice, confidence, or comfort with a difficult task. The chapter notes that people may mask personal limitations because admitting that they cannot do something can feel embarrassing, especially when the task is considered a basic part of the job. A person may point to other causes rather than disclose uncertainty. The leader should therefore ask respectfully about the person’s comfort, knowledge, and skill, and create conditions in which admitting a gap does not trigger humiliation.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a task that requires specialized knowledge or interpersonal skill. List the competencies required and the questions that would reveal a gap without shaming the person."
Key point:
- Source 2 concerns the individual’s knowledge and skill.
- Personal ability gaps may be hidden because people fear embarrassment.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
24. Q: How can a leader identify a personal skill or knowledge gap?
A: Show answer
The leader can ask how comfortable the person feels performing the task, what parts are clear or unclear, and where additional knowledge or practice would help. Questions should be calm and specific rather than implying that the person should already know everything. For example, a leader might ask which part of a difficult client interaction feels least manageable or what training would increase confidence. The leader should also examine observable evidence: repeated errors in a particular step, reliance on others for routine tasks, or uncertainty about a process. The purpose is not to label the employee but to determine what learning or support would make competent performance possible.
Study instruction:
- "Develop three neutral questions for assessing a skill gap. Pair each question with a possible response and a practical support option such as training, practice, or coaching."
Key point:
- Ask about comfort, knowledge, and skill directly but respectfully.
- Use observed task difficulties to target support rather than making global judgments.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
25. Q: Why might someone conceal a personal ability problem?
A: Show answer
A person may fear appearing incompetent, losing status, disappointing a manager, or admitting difficulty with something considered a basic job responsibility. The chapter’s medical-student example shows how powerful this pressure can become when a senior authority figure asks whether a junior person can perform a high-stakes task. The junior person may say nothing rather than risk embarrassment. Concealment deprives the team of information needed to protect quality and safety. Leaders should make it clear that raising a capability concern is responsible behavior, not a character defect, and that help will be directed toward resolving the gap.
Study instruction:
- "Identify status or embarrassment pressures in a team you know. Consider what a leader could say before assigning a task to make it easier to disclose a limitation."
Key point:
- Fear of embarrassment can hide critical ability information.
- Leaders must normalize candid disclosure of skill and knowledge limits.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
26. Q: What is a social ability barrier, or Source 4?
A: Show answer
A social ability barrier arises when the actions, omissions, cooperation, or support of other people affect whether the individual can perform the expected behavior. The task may depend on a colleague providing data, a supervisor granting access, a team coordinating handoffs, or an experienced coworker offering guidance. The person may have the necessary personal skill and equipment but still be unable to finish because another person’s contribution is missing. The chapter notes that these barriers can be easier to discuss than personal shortcomings, but people may hesitate to describe coworkers’ behavior because they do not want to appear to be reporting or blaming colleagues.
Study instruction:
- "Map the people whose actions are necessary for a task to succeed. Identify each dependency, what the person must provide, and how delays or omissions affect completion."
Key point:
- Source 4 concerns the enabling or hindering role of other people.
- A capable employee can still be blocked by an incomplete social dependency.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
27. Q: How should a leader discuss coworkers who may be contributing to an ability barrier?
A: Show answer
The leader should make it safe to discuss the contribution of others without turning the conversation into a search for someone to punish. The chapter warns that employees may protect friends or colleagues by blaming other factors if they fear being seen as a “rat.” The leader should explain that the purpose is to understand the workflow and remove barriers, not assign blame or seek retribution. Ask what cooperation is needed, where handoffs are breaking down, and what support would help. Focus on observable actions and dependencies rather than personal attacks or speculation about motives.
Study instruction:
- "Rewrite a blaming statement about a coworker into a description of a work dependency. Practise asking what coordination or support would make the task possible."
Key point:
- Discuss social barriers without adopting a find-the-guilty tone.
- Focus on actions, handoffs, and support rather than character judgments.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
28. Q: What role can a leader play in removing social ability barriers?
A: Show answer
The leader may be able to coordinate people, clarify responsibilities, obtain cooperation, arrange coaching or job shadowing, or provide direct support that the individual cannot secure alone. The chapter specifically encourages leaders to consider how they themselves could help socially. This is important because a barrier may persist not through the employee’s lack of effort but because the surrounding team has not aligned its work. A leader should identify who needs to do what, make the dependency visible, and agree on how assistance will be provided. The aim is to build a cooperative environment in which the required behavior is realistically achievable.
Study instruction:
- "For a cross-functional task, identify one action the leader can take to improve cooperation or coordination. Specify the person responsible and the point at which the support is needed."
Key point:
- Leaders can actively supply or coordinate social support.
- Clarifying dependencies and responsibilities can remove barriers outside the individual’s control.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
29. Q: What is a structural ability barrier, or Source 6?
A: Show answer
A structural ability barrier is created by the physical or organizational conditions in which work takes place. The chapter includes systems, work layout, policies, procedures, tools, and resources among the factors leaders should examine. It also prompts leaders to consider the AI-enabled tools people now rely on. A person may know how to perform a task and have willing colleagues, yet still face unnecessary obstacles because the system is cumbersome, the tool is unavailable, or the procedure makes the wrong action easier than the right one. Structural barriers are often accepted as permanent simply because “things have always been this way,” but they should be examined as possible causes of the gap.
Study instruction:
- "Inspect a process from beginning to end. Note the systems, tools, policies, physical layout, and procedures that either enable the desired behavior or add unnecessary steps."
Key point:
- Source 6 concerns the systems and environment surrounding the work.
- Inherited processes should be examined rather than assumed to be unchangeable.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
30. Q: Why are structural barriers sometimes overlooked?
A: Show answer
People often treat the surrounding environment as a fixed fact of life. Because a system, procedure, or work layout has existed for a long time, they may not think to question its effect on performance. The chapter notes that people can also forget how strongly the physical world influences behavior. A cumbersome process may become so familiar that its friction is invisible. Leaders can prompt fresh thinking by asking about tools, systems, policies, procedures, and work arrangements. This shifts the discussion from “Why won’t the person do it?” to “What about the environment makes the desired action harder than it needs to be?”
Study instruction:
- "Identify one long-standing procedure that people rarely question. Trace how it affects the time, effort, or likelihood of completing the desired behavior."
Key point:
- Familiar systems can conceal avoidable friction.
- Ask explicitly about the environment instead of treating it as fixed.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
31. Q: How can tools and technology create or remove structural ability barriers?
A: Show answer
Tools and technology affect how much effort is required to perform a behavior. An unavailable, unreliable, confusing, or poorly configured tool can obstruct the desired action even when the person has the right skills. Conversely, a suitable tool can simplify a task, reduce repeated effort, or provide a reminder at the moment it is needed. The chapter specifically invites leaders to consider the AI-enabled tools people now use, alongside ordinary systems and equipment. Technology should be evaluated by whether it genuinely removes the barrier and supports the required work, not by novelty alone. Leaders should also ensure that the person knows how to use the tool.
Study instruction:
- "Select one technology-dependent task. Identify whether the tool is accessible, fit for purpose, understood by the user, and integrated into the workflow."
Key point:
- Technology is part of the structural environment that shapes behavior.
- A tool helps only when it is suitable, accessible, and usable.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
32. Q: What does the chapter’s reminder about making the wrong behavior effortless imply for process design?
A: Show answer
The chapter asks leaders to notice whether the environment makes the undesired behavior easier than the desired one. If bypassing a required step takes no effort while following the correct process is cumbersome, people are likely to gravitate toward the easier path. This is not necessarily evidence that they reject the standard; the process may be designed in a way that continually adds friction to compliance. Leaders should therefore examine the relative effort of the available actions and redesign avoidable obstacles where possible. The standard still matters, but it should be supported by a process that makes the right action practical.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the number of steps and effort required for the correct process and its common shortcut. Identify one structural change that would make the desired behavior easier."
Key point:
- Process design influences which behavior is easiest to perform.
- Make the correct action practical rather than relying solely on reminders or pressure.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
33. Q: How should a leader use training to address a personal ability barrier?
A: Show answer
Training is appropriate when the person lacks knowledge or skill that can reasonably be developed through instruction and practice. The chapter gives the example of arranging training to help someone become more comfortable dealing with frustrated clients. Training should be linked to the specific capability gap rather than offered as a generic response to poor performance. The leader should clarify what the person needs to learn, select a suitable learning opportunity, and determine how the skill will be practised in the actual work. Training alone may not be enough if social or structural barriers remain, so the leader should also examine whether coaching, tools, or process changes are needed.
Study instruction:
- "Define a specific learning outcome for a real performance gap. Decide how the employee will practise the skill and how improvement will be observed in the work."
Key point:
- Training should target an identified knowledge or skill gap.
- Check whether other ability barriers could still prevent the trained person from succeeding.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
34. Q: When might coaching or job shadowing be more useful than formal training?
A: Show answer
Coaching or job shadowing can help when a person needs practical guidance, feedback, or exposure to how an experienced colleague handles a task. The chapter suggests combining training with support from other people, such as coaching or job shadowing. Formal instruction may explain a concept, while observation and guided practice help the person apply it in a real situation. These approaches also address social ability by making another person’s expertise available. The choice should depend on the actual barrier: a knowledge gap may call for training, while difficulty applying a skill in context may benefit from coaching or observation.
Study instruction:
- "For a task that someone understands but struggles to execute, decide whether coaching, job shadowing, practice, or formal training best addresses the difficulty. Explain why."
Key point:
- Different learning barriers call for different forms of support.
- Coaching and job shadowing can connect knowledge to real performance.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
35. Q: How can a simple reminder reduce a structural ability barrier?
A: Show answer
A reminder can reduce the mental effort required to remember a time-sensitive action. The chapter illustrates this with a young person who struggles to remember when to leave friends and get home. Setting an alarm on a phone before the required time creates an external prompt, reducing the need for repeated calls from a parent. The reminder does not remove the expectation or make the decision for the person; it changes the environment so the desired behavior is easier to remember. Similar prompts can support workplace handoffs, deadlines, checklists, and recurring steps when forgetting is the actual barrier.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a recurring missed step that is genuinely caused by forgetting. Design a timely reminder and decide how to determine whether it reduces the missed behavior."
Key point:
- A well-timed prompt can make follow-through easier.
- Use reminders to address memory or timing friction, not as a substitute for every other diagnosis.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
36. Q: How should a leader decide which ability barrier to address first?
A: Show answer
The chapter calls for brainstorming all possible sources rather than prescribing a universal order among personal, social, and structural barriers. A practical sequence is to identify each plausible obstacle, determine which ones actually prevent the behavior, and consider which can be removed most directly without creating new problems. A critical dependency may need immediate attention even if it is not the easiest issue to fix. The leader and employee should prioritize barriers based on their effect on the task and agree on concrete actions. After one barrier is addressed, they should reassess whether other barriers remain, because a single unresolved obstacle can still block success.
Study instruction:
- "Create a barrier list for a real task. Rank each item by its impact on completion, the evidence supporting it, and the feasibility of removing it."
Key point:
- Explore all three ability categories before settling on a remedy.
- Prioritize barriers by their actual effect on performance and verify that the remedy works.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
37. Q: What should a leader do when a person identifies several ability barriers at once?
A: Show answer
The leader should resist dismissing the list as excuses or trying to solve every item immediately. Clarify each barrier, determine how it affects the required behavior, and distinguish essential dependencies from secondary inconveniences. Ask the person which obstacle most prevents progress and what would help. Some barriers may require action by the leader, others by the employee, and others by a team or process owner. Agree on a manageable sequence, owners, and timing. If the task is urgent or safety-critical, address the barrier that creates the greatest immediate risk first. Continue checking for remaining barriers rather than assuming that one improvement resolves everything.
Study instruction:
- "Practise converting a long list of complaints into a prioritized action plan with a named owner and a specific next step for each critical barrier."
Key point:
- Acknowledge multiple barriers and separate them into actionable issues.
- Assign responsibility for removing each barrier instead of leaving the list unresolved.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
38. Q: How can a leader tell whether a proposed solution actually removes friction?
A: Show answer
A solution should change the condition that made the desired behavior difficult. The leader can ask the person to describe how the work will proceed after the change, what resource or support will now be available, and what could still prevent completion. A practical test is to compare the task before and after the intervention: Is the missing information available? Is the necessary colleague engaged? Can the person access and use the tool? Has the skill gap been addressed? The leader should then observe the next performance opportunity and invite feedback. A solution that sounds sensible but leaves the original obstacle intact has not removed the friction.
Study instruction:
- "For one proposed intervention, state the original barrier, the condition the intervention changes, and the observable evidence that the barrier has been reduced."
Key point:
- A remedy must alter the actual condition that blocked performance.
- Verify the result in the next real work cycle rather than relying on agreement alone.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
39. Q: What does the chapter teach about making it safe to admit an ability limitation?
A: Show answer
The chapter treats safety as a responsibility of the person leading the accountability discussion. People may hesitate to admit that they do not know how to perform a task, especially when the task is associated with their role or status. The leader should respond in a way that makes disclosure possible: remain calm, ask about comfort and skill, avoid humiliation, and focus on support. Safety does not mean lowering standards or pretending the capability gap is unimportant. It means ensuring that people can reveal what they cannot yet do so that the gap can be addressed before it causes further failure or harm.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare language that explicitly invites someone to disclose uncertainty without fear of ridicule. Practise responding constructively when the person says they need help."
Key point:
- Leaders are responsible for making ability barriers discussable.
- Psychological safety supports honest diagnosis while expectations remain clear.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
- Crucial Learning: Crucial Accountability Reader Resources
40. Q: What is the significance of the medical-student example in the chapter?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a senior surgeon who was called away during a procedure and asked a third-year medical student whether he could handle inserting an intravenous line into a patient’s chest. The student was uncomfortable admitting that he might not be able to perform the task and attempted it anyway. He punctured the sac around the patient’s lung, and the patient later died of related complications. The chapter uses this tragedy to show that a person’s reluctance to disclose a capability gap can have severe consequences, especially when authority and status make it difficult to speak up. The leader’s responsibility is to create conditions in which limitations are surfaced before unsafe action occurs.
Study instruction:
- "Analyse the authority relationship, the student’s fear of embarrassment, and the missed opportunity to clarify competence. Identify what a safe response to the surgeon’s question should have enabled."
Key point:
- Silence about ability can create serious safety risks.
- A leader must make it possible to say when a task is beyond one’s current competence.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
41. Q: What should a leader do when a task is safety-critical and the person is unsure they can perform it?
A: Show answer
The leader should treat uncertainty as information that must be addressed before the task proceeds. The person should be able to state their limits without being shamed or pressured into pretending competence. The leader must clarify what the task requires, confirm whether the person is qualified and prepared, and arrange appropriate supervision, training, or a competent alternative when needed. The chapter’s medical example illustrates the danger of allowing status pressure to override honest disclosure. In safety-critical work, making it easy to discuss ability is not merely a development practice; it is part of preventing avoidable harm. Applicable professional protocols and organizational safety requirements must also be followed.
Study instruction:
- "Use a safety-critical scenario to practise stopping, clarifying competence, and arranging qualified support. Identify the relevant formal procedures that govern the task."
Key point:
- Do not let embarrassment or hierarchy substitute for competence checks.
- Escalate or reassign work when the required capability is not established.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
42. Q: Why does the chapter say that caring is essential to addressing ability barriers?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that the way a leader handles ability reveals their intent. If the leader’s only goal is rapid compliance, they are unlikely to care whether the person has the knowledge, support, or conditions needed to succeed. If the leader genuinely wants the person to improve and the work to succeed, they will take time to understand and remove barriers. Caring is therefore not just a warm tone; it is demonstrated through concrete actions such as listening, arranging training, coordinating help, or improving a process. The expectation remains important, but the leader’s purpose includes enabling the person rather than merely extracting compliance.
Study instruction:
- "Review a recent accountability conversation and assess whether your actions showed a genuine interest in removing barriers. Identify one practical support action that would demonstrate care."
Key point:
- Intent is revealed by whether the leader takes ability barriers seriously.
- Care is demonstrated through useful support, not merely reassuring words.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
43. Q: Why does addressing ability first not mean ignoring motivation?
A: Show answer
The chapter warns that ability and motivation must both be examined. People may identify an ability barrier because it is less threatening than admitting that they are not willing to do what is expected. Once the stated ability barrier is removed, the person may still lack motivation. If the leader stops at the first explanation, the original performance gap may persist and the leader may mistakenly believe that the issue has been solved. The chapter therefore recommends beginning with ability and then checking the motivation side. The sequence makes it easier to remove genuine obstacles without treating an ability explanation as conclusive evidence of willingness.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why an ability explanation should be taken seriously but not treated as the final diagnosis. Write a follow-up question that tests willingness after support is arranged."
Key point:
- Ability and motivation are separate dimensions that can coexist.
- Resolving ability does not automatically establish motivation.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
44. Q: Why might a person point to an ability barrier even when motivation is also a problem?
A: Show answer
The chapter notes that admitting an ability problem can feel less threatening than acknowledging unwillingness. A person may find it easier to say that they lack a resource, information, or support than to discuss reluctance to perform the task. This does not mean every stated barrier is an excuse; genuine ability issues should be investigated respectfully. It means the leader should avoid stopping the diagnosis too early. After addressing the obstacle, the leader should ask whether the person is willing to complete the expected action under the improved conditions. That question helps distinguish an unresolved capability issue from a remaining motivation issue.
Study instruction:
- "Consider a case where a person repeatedly identifies new obstacles. Plan how to validate each real barrier while eventually checking willingness to act once the barriers are removed."
Key point:
- A less threatening explanation may conceal a separate motivation issue.
- Validate ability barriers and then assess willingness directly.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
45. Q: What does “check both sides” mean in an accountability conversation?
A: Show answer
“Check both sides” means examining ability and motivation rather than treating either as the whole explanation. The chapter recommends addressing ability first: identify and remove personal, social, and structural barriers. Then check whether the person is willing to meet the expectation once those barriers have been addressed. This prevents the leader from confusing capability with commitment. It also avoids the opposite error of pressuring someone who genuinely cannot perform. The two checks should be connected to the same clear expectation and a realistic set of conditions, so that the person can respond meaningfully rather than agree to an abstract demand.
Study instruction:
- "Role-play a conversation in which you first diagnose ability and then ask about motivation. Make sure the expectation and the proposed support are both specific."
Key point:
- Ability is addressed first, then motivation is checked.
- Both dimensions are necessary for a complete diagnosis of a performance gap.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
46. Q: How can a leader check motivation after removing an ability barrier?
A: Show answer
The leader can describe the support or changed condition and ask whether the person is willing to complete the task under those conditions. The chapter gives an example: “If I get the draft to you by 2 p.m., are you willing to finish the job by 5 p.m., or is there something else I need to know?” The question is useful because it makes the condition concrete and invites the person to disclose any remaining concern. The leader should listen carefully to the response, clarify what is still uncertain, and avoid treating a quick “yes” as proof if the person’s words or circumstances suggest otherwise.
Study instruction:
- "Adapt the chapter’s draft-by-2 p.m. question to a real task. Specify the support being offered, the required outcome, and the time by which willingness must be confirmed."
Key point:
- Ask about willingness under the improved conditions, not in the abstract.
- A concrete follow-up question can expose remaining concerns.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
47. Q: What should a leader do if the person says they are willing but still cannot complete the task?
A: Show answer
A stated willingness does not prove that all ability barriers have been removed. The leader should revisit the three ability categories and ask what remains difficult. There may be an overlooked personal skill gap, a social dependency that has not been secured, or a structural obstacle that continues to add friction. The leader should also check whether the proposed support was delivered as agreed. Avoid immediately interpreting another failure as dishonesty or lack of effort. Use the result as evidence to refine the diagnosis, adjust the support, and set a clear next commitment. If the barriers have genuinely been removed, motivation can then be explored more directly.
Study instruction:
- "Design a follow-up review that compares promised support with support actually received. Identify what new evidence would indicate a remaining personal, social, or structural barrier."
Key point:
- Willingness and capability must be verified in practice.
- Reassess the barrier model when performance still does not improve.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
48. Q: How should a leader respond when the person has the ability but is not willing?
A: Show answer
Once genuine ability barriers have been addressed and the person can perform the task, the leader should not continue treating the problem as a capability issue. The remaining discussion needs to address motivation and the person’s willingness to meet the expectation. The chapter’s contribution is the diagnostic sequence: first make it possible, then check whether the person is willing. A leader should be clear about the expectation and invite an honest response about what is preventing commitment. The appropriate next steps depend on the wider accountability process, the nature of the expectation, and applicable organizational policies; the chapter does not suggest that support replaces the need to address unwillingness.
Study instruction:
- "Create a scenario in which a person has the necessary skills and resources but declines to follow through. Identify how the conversation changes after ability has been verified."
Key point:
- Do not keep prescribing training when the remaining issue is willingness.
- Once ability is established, address motivation and commitment clearly.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
49. Q: How can leaders avoid confusing an excuse with a genuine ability barrier?
A: Show answer
The chapter does not recommend dismissing explanations as excuses. Instead, it encourages leaders to investigate them. Ask what specifically prevents the task, what has been tried, which people or resources are needed, and what would change the situation. A genuine barrier should have a concrete relationship to the behavior and a plausible remedy. The leader can then test the explanation by arranging the support and observing whether the person is able to proceed. At the same time, the leader should check motivation after the barrier is removed. This approach avoids both cynicism toward legitimate obstacles and naïveté about the possibility of unresolved unwillingness.
Study instruction:
- "Take an explanation you might instinctively label an excuse. Translate it into a testable barrier hypothesis and define what evidence would confirm or disconfirm it."
Key point:
- Investigate explanations instead of accepting or rejecting them reflexively.
- A practical test is whether removing the stated barrier enables the required behavior.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
50. Q: How can a leader combine personal, social, and structural solutions in one plan?
A: Show answer
A single performance gap may require several complementary interventions. A person may need training to build a skill, coaching from an experienced colleague to apply it, and access to a simpler system to complete the work efficiently. The chapter’s examples show that ability support can span Sources 2, 4, and 6. A useful plan names each barrier, the intervention, the responsible person, and the point at which the support will be available. The leader should not assume that training alone solves a workflow dependency or that a new tool automatically creates competence. The combined plan should make the required behavior realistically achievable.
Study instruction:
- "Build a three-source support plan for a difficult task. Include one personal, one social, and one structural action, with owners and evidence of completion."
Key point:
- Complex gaps may need more than one kind of ability support.
- Coordinate the interventions so that one unresolved barrier does not defeat the plan.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
51. Q: What are common leadership mistakes when responding to an ability problem?
A: Show answer
The chapter highlights several errors: assuming the problem is motivation, jumping in with a solution before hearing the person’s ideas, presenting the leader’s idea first and biasing the response, pretending to involve the person while steering them toward a predetermined answer, and acting as though a leader must know everything. Other mistakes include looking only at personal skill while ignoring social and structural barriers, creating a blaming atmosphere around coworkers, and failing to check motivation after ability is addressed. These errors either hide useful information, weaken ownership, or leave the actual obstacle unresolved. A disciplined conversation replaces quick judgment with joint diagnosis and practical follow-through.
Study instruction:
- "Review a recent difficult conversation against this list. Select the mistake most likely to recur and write a specific behavior you will use to prevent it."
Key point:
- Premature solutions and narrow diagnoses leave barriers hidden.
- Authentic involvement, broad diagnosis, and a second motivation check improve accountability.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
52. Q: How can a leader use the chapter’s approach with a remote or hybrid team?
A: Show answer
The same ability categories apply even when colleagues are not physically together. Personal barriers may include uncertainty about a digital process or lack of skill with a tool. Social barriers may arise when handoffs, responses, or access to a colleague’s expertise are delayed. Structural barriers may include confusing software, inaccessible files, fragmented communication channels, or procedures that make the desired action difficult. The leader should ask the person for ideas, map the dependencies, and examine the digital work environment rather than assuming that distance is simply a motivation problem. The chapter’s invitation to consider modern AI-enabled tools reinforces the need to assess the actual technology people use.
Study instruction:
- "Map a remote workflow from request to completion. Identify personal, social, and structural friction points and propose one practical change in each category."
Key point:
- The ability model applies to digital and distributed work.
- Remote performance depends on skills, coordination, and the design of tools and processes.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
53. Q: How can a leader apply “Make It Easy” to a recurring process failure rather than a one-time missed task?
A: Show answer
For a recurring failure, the leader should look for a persistent barrier embedded in the person’s skills, team relationships, or work system. Repeatedly reminding the employee to try harder may leave the underlying friction untouched. Ask the people involved where the process breaks down, identify recurring handoff or tool problems, and examine whether the correct behavior is unnecessarily difficult. A recurring issue may require training, clearer coordination, a redesigned procedure, or a more usable tool. After implementing a change, monitor whether the failure rate or pattern changes. If the same gap continues despite verified ability, revisit motivation and the wider accountability expectations.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a recurring process failure and trace it across several instances. Separate individual errors from recurring system conditions and define a way to evaluate whether the intervention works."
Key point:
- Recurring gaps often require attention to the conditions that repeatedly produce them.
- Monitor the process after removing friction and reopen the diagnosis if the pattern persists.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
54. Q: How should a leader document an ability-removal agreement?
A: Show answer
The chapter emphasizes joint diagnosis and workable solutions; a practical way to preserve that agreement is to record the identified barrier, the action that will remove it, who will provide the support, when it will be available, and what outcome is expected. Documentation should not turn a supportive conversation into bureaucracy. It helps both parties remember the agreed conditions and makes follow-through observable. For example, record that the manager will arrange access to a qualified reviewer by a specified time and that the employee will submit the completed draft after receiving the required data. The record should also capture any unresolved dependency that needs further attention.
Study instruction:
- "Draft a concise action record for a barrier-removal conversation. Include the barrier, support action, owner, due time, expected behavior, and review point."
Key point:
- Specific agreements make support and responsibility visible.
- Record both the leader’s enabling actions and the employee’s next commitment.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
55. Q: How can a leader preserve accountability while providing support?
A: Show answer
Providing support does not mean removing the expectation or taking over the employee’s responsibility. The leader helps make the task feasible by addressing barriers, while the person remains involved in planning and carrying out the required behavior. A balanced agreement states what support will be provided and what the person will do once that support is available. The leader should avoid both extremes: blaming the person for conditions outside their control and assuming full responsibility for work that the person can reasonably own. The chapter’s approach treats accountability as a partnership in solving the gap, not as a choice between demanding results and being supportive.
Study instruction:
- "Write a two-sided commitment: one action the leader will take to remove friction and one action the employee will take to meet the expectation. Make both observable."
Key point:
- Support and accountability are complementary, not competing.
- The leader removes barriers while the individual retains appropriate ownership.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
56. Q: What is the relationship between ability support and sustainable accountability?
A: Show answer
Sustainable accountability requires more than securing a single instance of compliance. If the conditions that made the task difficult remain unchanged, the same gap is likely to recur. By helping people identify and remove personal, social, and structural barriers, leaders improve the possibility that the desired behavior can be repeated. Involving the person in the solution also supports ownership and commitment. The chapter therefore presents ability support as a way to foster lasting change, not as a concession made only to resolve one missed deadline. Leaders should verify that the support works and that motivation is also present.
Study instruction:
- "Compare a one-time rescue with a change that makes future performance easier. Identify which intervention is more likely to prevent the same gap from recurring and why."
Key point:
- Sustainable change requires improved conditions, not only immediate compliance.
- Jointly remove friction and check both ability and motivation.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
57. Q: How can a leader tell whether they are acting as a facilitator rather than a rescuer?
A: Show answer
A facilitator helps the person understand the barrier, generate options, obtain needed support, and own the next action. A rescuer takes over the thinking and often the work itself. The chapter encourages leaders to see themselves as facilitators, enablers, and supporters rather than drill sergeants or cheerleaders. A facilitator asks before advising, shares expertise transparently, and makes the person a partner in the solution. They do not withhold necessary direction when safety or policy requires it, but they avoid creating unnecessary dependence. The test is whether the person becomes more capable of solving the problem, not merely whether the leader fixes the immediate issue.
Study instruction:
- "After helping someone solve a problem, ask what they learned and what they would do if the same barrier appeared again. Use the answer to assess whether ownership has grown."
Key point:
- Facilitation develops the person’s ability to solve future problems.
- Rescuing may close today’s gap while increasing dependence tomorrow.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
58. Q: What questions can a leader use to brainstorm personal, social, and structural ability barriers?
A: Show answer
For personal ability, ask what knowledge, skill, practice, or confidence the task requires and which part feels difficult. For social ability, ask whose cooperation, information, coaching, or decision is needed and where a handoff is failing. For structural ability, ask whether the tools, systems, policies, procedures, resources, or work layout make the desired action harder. These questions should be asked as an open exploration rather than a checklist used to interrogate the employee. The leader should invite the person to add possibilities, clarify which barriers are real, and decide together what would make the task easier.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a short question set for each of the three ability categories. Use it in a role-play without suggesting answers before the other person has a chance to respond."
Key point:
- Use questions tailored to personal, social, and structural conditions.
- A broad inquiry prevents the leader from overlooking an entire source of friction.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
59. Q: How should a leader respond when the person proposes a solution the leader believes is imperfect?
A: Show answer
The leader should first understand the reasoning behind the proposal rather than dismiss it immediately. Because effectiveness depends on both accuracy and commitment, the leader needs to evaluate the technical quality of the idea while preserving genuine involvement. Ask what problem the proposal addresses, what evidence supports it, and what risks or dependencies it creates. If it is workable but not the strongest option, explain the trade-off and explore whether it can be improved. If it is unsafe, ineffective, or inconsistent with a non-negotiable requirement, be transparent about that constraint and invite ideas about an acceptable alternative.
Study instruction:
- "Take a proposed solution that is not your first choice. Practise evaluating its accuracy, implementation commitment, risks, and possible improvements before deciding."
Key point:
- Respect participation while testing the solution’s quality.
- Be honest about constraints rather than pretending every suggestion can be adopted.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
60. Q: What should happen when a proposed ability solution depends on another person or department?
A: Show answer
The dependency should be made explicit and converted into an agreed action rather than left as a vague hope. Identify who must provide what, by when, and how the employee will know the support is available. If the dependency is outside the employee’s authority, the leader may need to coordinate or escalate it. The Akira scenario demonstrates that an employee can be blocked by the unavailability of a specialist even when the employee has made substantial progress. A practical plan therefore addresses the dependency itself, not merely the employee’s effort. The leader should also identify a contingency if the expected support does not materialize.
Study instruction:
- "For a cross-department task, document the dependency, provider, required input, deadline, confirmation method, and contingency plan."
Key point:
- External dependencies must be actively managed.
- A commitment is not feasible until essential contributions are secured or replaced.

- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
61. Q: How can leaders distinguish a structural barrier from an individual performance weakness?
A: Show answer
A structural barrier is indicated when the surrounding process or environment makes the desired behavior difficult for people who otherwise have the necessary knowledge and willingness. An individual performance weakness is more likely when the person lacks a specific skill or fails to apply it despite suitable conditions. These explanations can coexist. Leaders should examine whether others performing the same task encounter the same obstacle, whether the process contains unnecessary steps, and whether tools or access are consistently available. They should also ask the individual what part of the task they find difficult. The aim is to avoid blaming a person for a system problem or overlooking an individual learning need.
Study instruction:
- "Compare how two people experience the same workflow. Identify evidence that points to a common structural obstacle versus a person-specific skill gap."
Key point:
- Look for patterns across people and process conditions.
- Do not attribute a system-generated difficulty solely to individual effort.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
62. Q: What is the danger of treating the physical or organizational environment as unchangeable?
A: Show answer
When people assume that “things have always been this way,” they may stop noticing that the environment is making good performance unnecessarily difficult. The same cumbersome form, approval path, tool, or layout can continue creating missed steps and delays because no one questions it. The chapter encourages leaders to prompt people to think about these conditions, including policies, procedures, systems, work layout, and tools. Not every constraint can be changed, and some exist for legitimate reasons. But treating all existing arrangements as fixed prevents the team from discovering improvements that could make the desired behavior easier and more reliable.
Study instruction:
- "Identify one accepted workplace constraint and ask whether it is legally, operationally, or procedurally necessary. If it is not, propose a controlled way to test an improvement."
Key point:
- Tradition is not evidence that a process is optimal.
- Question inherited friction while preserving legitimate controls and requirements.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
63. Q: How should a leader respond when the person says, “I have the tools, but I still do not feel comfortable doing this”?
A: Show answer
The response should shift attention toward personal ability rather than assuming that equipment solves the problem. Ask which part of the task feels uncomfortable, whether the person understands the process, and what knowledge or practice would increase confidence. The chapter’s example explicitly contrasts having resources and tools with having the necessary knowledge and skills. The leader might arrange training, practice, coaching, or job shadowing, depending on the gap. They should also check whether social support is needed during the learning period. The objective is to make competent performance possible, not to pressure the person into concealing uncertainty.
Study instruction:
- "Create a support plan for someone who has the required equipment but lacks confidence or skill. Specify the learning method and how competence will be confirmed."
Key point:
- Having tools does not prove personal ability.
- Address knowledge, skill, and comfort when equipment alone is insufficient.

- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
64. Q: How can a leader use the chapter’s approach when a person repeatedly misses a deadline?
A: Show answer
Begin by clarifying the expected result and understanding what happened, then explore whether the deadline is blocked by personal, social, or structural ability barriers. The person may lack a required skill, depend on late information from another team, or be working with an inefficient system. Ask for their ideas before prescribing a remedy. Agree on what support will remove the barrier and what the person will deliver once it is available. Then check willingness under those conditions. If the person has the ability and support but remains unwilling to meet the commitment, the discussion must move beyond ability. The chapter’s sequence helps prevent both unfair blame and endless acceptance of unresolved explanations.
Study instruction:
- "Apply the sequence to a real late deliverable: clarify the gap, diagnose all ability sources, agree on support, confirm willingness, and set a review point."
Key point:
- Repeated lateness requires diagnosis, not automatic assumptions about effort.
- Support must be paired with a clear commitment and a motivation check.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
65. Q: How can a leader prevent the two skills from becoming a mechanical checklist?
A: Show answer
The two skills—asking for ideas and brainstorming all sources—are intended to guide genuine problem-solving, not replace judgment with a rigid script. The leader should adapt questions to the person, task, risk, and context. In a routine task, a brief inquiry may be sufficient. In a high-stakes situation, the leader may need to slow down, verify competence, and follow formal safeguards. The leader should listen for what the person is actually saying, pursue relevant details, and remain open to a barrier outside the initial categories considered. The model provides structure while the conversation remains human, specific, and responsive.
Study instruction:
- "Practise using the model in two contrasting situations: a low-risk administrative delay and a high-risk technical task. Identify what should change in the depth and urgency of the inquiry."
Key point:
- Use the model as a guide to inquiry, not a script to recite.
- Adjust the depth of diagnosis to the task’s complexity and risk.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
66. Q: What is a practical end-to-end sequence for applying “Make It Easy”?
A: Show answer
First, describe the performance gap clearly and respectfully. Second, invite the person’s ideas about what needs to happen. Third, brainstorm personal, social, and structural ability barriers. Fourth, determine which barriers are supported by the facts and which can be removed. Fifth, agree on specific actions, owners, and timing, including support the leader must provide. Sixth, check whether the person is willing to meet the expectation once the barriers are addressed. Finally, follow up to see whether the new conditions actually enable the behavior and whether any barrier remains. This sequence keeps the conversation focused on both practical feasibility and the person’s commitment.
Study instruction:
- "Use this sequence to prepare for an upcoming accountability discussion. Write the expected behavior, possible barriers, open questions, support actions, willingness check, and follow-up point."
Key point:
- The method moves from candid gap description to joint diagnosis and barrier removal.
- Follow-through includes checking both ability and motivation.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
67. Q: How can the chapter’s approach improve a team’s problem-solving culture over time?
A: Show answer
When leaders consistently ask for ideas and treat ability barriers as legitimate topics, team members learn that problems should be surfaced and solved rather than hidden. People are more likely to contribute their knowledge, ask for help, and take responsibility for workable solutions. The team also becomes more attentive to dependencies and structural friction that affect multiple people. This can reduce repeated firefighting and reliance on a single manager to provide every answer. The cultural benefit depends on leaders acting consistently: they must listen, avoid staged participation, provide promised support, and still check commitments. A supportive culture is built through repeated evidence that candid problem-solving is valued.
Study instruction:
- "Identify one team routine where employees could be invited to diagnose friction and suggest improvements. Define how ideas will be evaluated and how the team will hear what was implemented."
Key point:
- Repeated genuine involvement can normalize early problem-solving.
- A supportive culture requires follow-through, not just invitations to speak.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
68. Q: What advanced lesson does the chapter offer about the relationship between leadership power and employee voice?
A: Show answer
A leader’s authority can unintentionally narrow what others are willing to say. Presenting a solution first, pretending to consult, or reacting harshly to an admission of limited skill can suppress information that the leader needs. The chapter’s advice—to ask first, admit uncertainty, and make ability barriers safe to discuss—helps counter this effect. The leader must use authority to create conditions for honest information rather than to secure agreement at any cost. This is especially important when the employee’s knowledge differs from the leader’s or when the employee sees a risk the leader has missed. Good accountability depends on hearing reality, not merely hearing compliance.
Study instruction:
- "Reflect on how your role or status may affect the answers people give you. Identify one behavior that would make disagreement or disclosure safer."
Key point:
- Authority can suppress the information needed for accurate diagnosis.
- Use leadership power to enable candor and joint problem-solving.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
69. Q: How should leaders evaluate whether an ability intervention has produced lasting improvement?
A: Show answer
The leader should look beyond whether the immediate task was completed. Check whether the person can now perform the behavior under the agreed conditions, whether the support is reliably available, and whether the same barrier recurs. Ask the person what changed and what remains difficult. If the intervention involved training, observe whether the skill transfers to actual work. If it involved a social dependency, confirm that the handoff or cooperation is functioning. If it involved a structural change, verify that the tool or process is being used as intended. Then check motivation and follow-through. Lasting improvement means the conditions and behavior have changed, not merely that one deadline was rescued.
Study instruction:
- "Define two outcome measures for an ability intervention: one showing that the barrier was removed and one showing that the desired behavior is sustained over time."
Key point:
- Evaluate both the intervention and the resulting behavior.
- A one-time success does not prove that the underlying friction has been removed permanently.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
70. Q: What is the most important takeaway from Chapter 7, “Make It Easy”?
A: Show answer
The chapter’s central message is that accountability is more effective when leaders help people become able to meet expectations rather than relying first on pressure. Start by asking the person for ideas instead of rushing to supply answers. Then brainstorm all ability barriers across personal, social, and structural sources. Make it safe to discuss limitations, dependencies, and system problems; involve the person honestly; and take practical action to remove the friction. Finally, check the motivation side once ability has been addressed. This approach respects people while keeping results and commitments in view. It treats accountability as the work of creating conditions for responsible performance, not simply demanding compliance.
Study instruction:
- "Summarize the chapter without notes using the phrases “ask for ideas,” “brainstorm all sources,” and “check both sides.” Apply each phrase to a real accountability situation."
Key point:
- Make the right behavior easier by jointly removing ability barriers.
- After addressing ability, confirm motivation and sustain the commitment.
Source links:
- Primary source: Crucial Accountability, 3rd ed., Chapter 7, “Make It Easy” (supplied chapter text).
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
