- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
- Crucial Learning: Crucial Conversations for Accountability
13. Q: What makes an action or deliverable sufficiently specific?
A: Show answer
It is sufficiently specific when the responsible person and requester share the same understanding of the expected output, its scope, and the conditions for completion. A deliverable may need a format, quality standard, audience, approval step, or boundary around permissible changes. The appropriate level of detail depends on task complexity and risk. The objective is not to document every trivial step but to remove ambiguity that could materially affect the result. If two reasonable people could interpret the instruction differently, clarify it before treating it as an agreement.
Study instruction:
- "Test a draft commitment by asking whether two people could reasonably interpret its scope or completion differently."
Key point:
- Specificity should eliminate material ambiguity about scope and completion.
- The required detail should be proportionate to the task's complexity and consequences.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
- Crucial Learning: Crucial Conversations for Accountability
14. Q: Why should deadlines be stated precisely?
A: Show answer
A precise deadline gives the responsible person and requester a common reference point for planning and assessing completion. Phrases such as 'next week,' 'soon,' or 'as quickly as possible' can mean different things to different people. The chapter illustrates that 'next week' may technically permit delivery late on Saturday even when the requester needs the work on Thursday afternoon. Explicit timing prevents a person from being judged against a deadline that was never clearly agreed.
Study instruction:
- "Replace relative timing phrases in current work plans with agreed dates and, where necessary, times."
Key point:
- A deadline should reflect the actual operational need, not an ambiguous calendar phrase.
- Fair accountability requires a deadline that was clearly communicated and agreed.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
- Crucial Learning: Crucial Conversations for Accountability
15. Q: How should the phrase 'next week' be clarified?
A: Show answer
Ask what specific day and time the work must be available, then confirm whether that is the delivery deadline or an interim checkpoint. If the requester needs a report by Thursday at 5:00 p.m., state that exact requirement rather than saying 'next week.' Also clarify whether the deadline refers to a draft, a reviewed version, or a final approved product. A broad calendar expression can be technically defensible yet operationally useless when the parties have different needs.
Study instruction:
- "Rewrite three vague deadline statements as exact dates, times, and delivery states."
Key point:
- Relative time expressions should be converted into specific dates and times.
- Clarify whether the deadline applies to a draft, final deliverable, or approval.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
- Crucial Learning: Crucial Conversations for Accountability
16. Q: How should a deadline be negotiated when the requested timing may not be feasible?
A: Show answer
The responsible person should explain the constraint early and propose a realistic alternative, while the requester explains the operational need behind the original deadline. Together they can consider scope, sequencing, resources, quality, or an interim deliverable. The agreement should identify the revised date and any milestones that protect the overall objective. A deadline should be mutually understood rather than silently accepted and later missed. A negotiated commitment is more useful than an enthusiastic but unrealistic promise.
Study instruction:
- "Practice negotiating a deadline by stating the constraint, its impact, and a concrete alternative."
Key point:
- A realistic negotiated commitment is stronger than an unworkable promise.
- Clarifying the underlying need can reveal alternatives to simply extending the deadline.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
- Crucial Learning: Crucial Conversations for Accountability
17. Q: What should be clarified when a task has more than one important date?
A: Show answer
Distinguish the start date, interim milestones, review dates, submission deadline, and final approval date. A person may complete a draft by the agreed date but still fail to meet the business need if review and correction time were not considered. The plan should also identify dependencies, such as when another team must provide data or approval. Applying the chapter's requirement for clear timing to multi-stage work means making material handoffs explicit rather than relying on one final deadline to govern the whole process.
Study instruction:
- "Map a multi-stage assignment and identify the dates at which work must be handed over or reviewed."
Key point:
- Multi-stage work needs clear timing for handoffs as well as final delivery.
- A deadline is only workable when dependencies and review time are considered.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
- Crucial Learning: Crucial Conversations for Accountability
18. Q: What is the purpose of the Follow-up element in WWWF?
A: Show answer
Follow-up establishes when and how the parties will check whether commitments are progressing and whether the agreed result has been achieved. It creates an opportunity to identify obstacles before a deadline is missed and to confirm whether the original plan remains workable. Follow-up is not simply surveillance or a final inspection. It is a deliberate agreement about communication and review, calibrated to the task and the person performing it. Without it, both parties may assume the other will initiate contact, and emerging problems can remain invisible until they become failures.
Study instruction:
- "For each commitment, specify a progress-check method and the event or date that will trigger it."
Key point:
- Follow-up makes progress visible before a problem becomes a missed commitment.
- The parties should agree in advance on who checks, when, and by what method.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
- Crucial Learning: Crucial Conversations for Accountability
19. Q: Which three variables should guide the frequency and type of follow-up?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies risk, trust, and competence. Risk concerns how consequential failure would be. Trust concerns the person's demonstrated reliability in meeting commitments. Competence concerns the person's experience and capability in the relevant area. These variables help determine how much support and monitoring are appropriate. A high-risk task assigned to someone with limited experience and a weak track record calls for earlier and more frequent checks. A routine task assigned to a capable and reliable person can usually be followed up more casually.
Study instruction:
- "Assess risk, trust, and competence separately before selecting a follow-up approach."
Key point:
- Risk, trust, and competence are the chapter's three follow-up calibration factors.
- The appropriate follow-up level depends on the combination of task and person.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
- Crucial Learning: Crucial Conversations for Accountability
20. Q: How does risk influence the follow-up plan?
A: Show answer
The greater the potential harm or consequence of failure, the more deliberate the follow-up should be. A critical safety, customer, regulatory, or operational deliverable may require early checkpoints and prompt escalation of delays. A low-impact routine task may need only a normal status review. Risk should be assessed in relation to the consequence of missing the commitment, not merely the apparent size of the task. The chapter treats risk as one of three variables that should shape follow-up frequency and method.
Study instruction:
- "Rank several current commitments by the consequence of failure and explain how the ranking changes follow-up."
Key point:
- High-consequence commitments warrant earlier visibility and more deliberate checks.
- Follow-up intensity should reflect the impact of failure, not simply task size.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
- Crucial Learning: Crucial Conversations for Accountability
21. Q: How should a person's track record affect follow-up?
A: Show answer
A history of reliable performance can support a lighter, less frequent follow-up arrangement, while repeated missed commitments justify closer and earlier checks. Trust should be based on observed performance rather than personal preference or assumptions about character. A strong track record does not eliminate the need for a clear agreement, and a weak track record does not justify disrespectful monitoring. The purpose is to match follow-up to evidence about reliability and the risk of the work.
Study instruction:
- "Use documented performance patterns, not general impressions, when deciding how closely to follow up."
Key point:
- Past reliability is relevant evidence when choosing the level of monitoring.
- Closer follow-up should remain respectful and tied to the work, not personal judgment.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
- Crucial Learning: Crucial Conversations for Accountability
22. Q: How does competence affect the amount of follow-up needed?
A: Show answer
A person experienced in the task may need less frequent intervention than someone unfamiliar with the work. An inexperienced person may benefit from earlier checkpoints, coaching, clarification, and help identifying dependencies. This is particularly important when the task is complex or mistakes are costly. The chapter advises more frequent follow-up when a critical task is assigned to someone inexperienced or with a poor track record. The aim is to make success more likely, not to label the person as incapable.
Study instruction:
- "Identify where a task is new to its owner and add an early support checkpoint."
Key point:
- Limited experience can increase the need for early guidance and progress checks.
- Follow-up should build the conditions for success rather than stigmatize inexperience.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
- Crucial Learning: Crucial Conversations for Accountability
23. Q: How should risk, trust, and competence be considered together?
A: Show answer
Assess all three rather than relying on only one. A highly competent person may still need close follow-up on a high-risk assignment. A trusted colleague may be new to a particular task and need coaching. A routine task may justify a light approach even when the person has limited experience, provided the consequences of failure are modest and support is available. The chapter's example of a critical assignment given to an inexperienced person with a poor track record illustrates why combined assessment points toward early and frequent follow-up.
Study instruction:
- "Create a brief follow-up rationale that records the risk, reliability, and experience factors for one assignment."
Key point:
- No single variable should determine follow-up in isolation.
- A critical task plus limited experience and weak reliability calls for close early support.

- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
- Crucial Learning: Crucial Conversations for Accountability
24. Q: What is a scheduled follow-up?
A: Show answer
A scheduled follow-up is a progress check arranged for a particular date or at a regular meeting. It works well for routine work because the parties can use an existing meeting or agreed review time to assess progress. The check should be purposeful: review what has been completed, what remains, whether the deadline is still realistic, and whether help is needed. Scheduling the check in advance prevents both parties from relying on memory or waiting until the final due date to discover that progress has stalled.
Study instruction:
- "Add a specific progress-review date to a routine commitment and define what will be reviewed."
Key point:
- Scheduled checks provide a predictable time to review progress.
- A useful review examines remaining work, deadline confidence, and support needs.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
- Crucial Learning: Crucial Conversations for Accountability
25. Q: What is an event-based follow-up?
A: Show answer
An event-based follow-up occurs when a defined milestone or event takes place rather than at a fixed calendar interval. For example, a person may agree to report back as soon as an initial plan is completed. This approach is useful when progress depends on meaningful stages or when the timing of those stages is not predictable enough for a simple calendar meeting. The event must be concrete enough that both parties know when the check is due and who is responsible for initiating it.
Study instruction:
- "For a complex assignment, identify a meaningful milestone that should trigger a progress conversation."
Key point:
- An event-based check is triggered by a defined milestone or event.
- The trigger and the person responsible for initiating contact must be unambiguous.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
- Crucial Learning: Crucial Conversations for Accountability
26. Q: When should scheduled and event-based follow-ups be combined?
A: Show answer
Combine them when the work has important milestones but also needs a backstop if a milestone is delayed. The chapter gives the pattern of asking for a report when the initial plan is complete and agreeing to meet if it has not been completed by a specified day. The event trigger encourages prompt communication when progress occurs; the calendar date prevents silence if it does not. This combination is useful when dependencies or uncertainty could otherwise cause the parties to lose contact.
Study instruction:
- "Design a milestone trigger and a fallback date for one complex assignment."
Key point:
- A milestone trigger encourages timely reporting when progress occurs.
- A calendar backstop prevents a delayed milestone from producing silence.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
- Crucial Learning: Crucial Conversations for Accountability
27. Q: How can a manager follow up without micromanaging?
A: Show answer
Agree on the purpose, timing, and form of checks in advance, and scale them to risk, trust, and competence. Ask for progress against milestones and obstacles rather than repeatedly requesting reassurance. Give the person room to decide how to perform the work within agreed boundaries. For routine work assigned to an experienced and reliable colleague, a normal meeting may be sufficient. For a critical task assigned to someone new to the work, closer early contact may be appropriate. The aim is effective support and accountability, not constant interruption.
Study instruction:
- "Compare a proportionate milestone review with unnecessary status requests and explain the difference."
Key point:
- Agreeing on follow-up in advance reduces arbitrary checking.
- Monitor meaningful progress and risks while preserving appropriate autonomy.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
- Crucial Learning: Crucial Conversations for Accountability
28. Q: What should happen if a person encounters an obstacle before the deadline?
A: Show answer
The parties should use the agreed follow-up channel to surface the obstacle early, assess its effect, and decide whether the plan needs adjustment. They may clarify a requirement, remove a dependency, obtain support, revise sequencing, or negotiate a new deadline. The person responsible should not wait silently until the due date if the obstacle threatens the commitment. The requester should also avoid treating every obstacle as a lack of motivation; first establish what has changed and what action will restore progress.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare an escalation message that states the obstacle, its impact, and the decision or support needed."
Key point:
- Early disclosure creates time to solve problems before the deadline.
- Diagnose the obstacle before deciding whether the issue is capability, priority, or planning.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition

29. Q: How can follow-up support problem-solving as well as accountability?
A: Show answer
A follow-up discussion can confirm progress and reveal that the original plan no longer fits the circumstances. If a milestone is missed, the parties can identify the cause, determine whether expectations were unclear, and agree on corrective action. If the person lacks a skill or resource, follow-up can expose that need before the final result fails. The chapter's product management example shows why follow-up should test whether concerns about capability and priorities have actually been addressed, rather than merely recording that people agreed with the concerns.
Study instruction:
- "At each checkpoint, ask both what has been completed and what is preventing the next step."
Key point:
- Follow-up is an opportunity to diagnose and remove barriers, not only inspect status.
- A plan should be adjusted when new information shows that it will not close the gap.

30. Q: Should follow-up commitments be assigned to both parties?
A: Show answer
Yes, when both parties have actions needed to close the gap. The chapter's real-life example asks who will do what, by when, and how follow-up will occur in relation to both the capability concern and the priority concern. A manager may need to arrange training, clarify authority, provide resources, or remove a barrier, while the employee or team carries out agreed work. Assigning only the other person tasks can create an incomplete plan if the requester also has responsibilities essential to success.
Study instruction:
- "List the actions required from each participant and give every action its own owner and due date."
Key point:
- The person raising the issue may also have enabling responsibilities.
- Reciprocal commitments make the solution practical rather than one-sided.

31. Q: When should an accountability agreement be written down?
A: Show answer
Written documentation is especially useful when work is complex, consequences are significant, several people are involved, or the agreement contains multiple actions and milestones. Chapter 9 encourages explicit clarity, even written clarity, rather than leaving details to memory. A concise record can capture the owner, deliverable, deadline, boundaries, follow-up method, and dependencies. The level of documentation should be proportionate to the situation. The purpose is to preserve shared understanding and make later review fair, not to create paperwork for its own sake.
Study instruction:
- "Draft a short written WWWF record for a commitment with several dependencies or participants."
Key point:
- Written records protect shared understanding when the plan is complex or consequential.
- Documentation should be proportionate and useful for follow-up.

32. Q: How should an accountability plan be documented so that it remains usable?
A: Show answer
Use plain, unambiguous language and record one commitment per action item. For each item, identify the owner, observable output or behavior, due date and time where needed, and follow-up trigger. Include important boundaries and dependencies, and note any actions the requester has accepted. Avoid vague status labels such as 'in progress' without describing the next milestone. Confirm that the other party agrees with the record and knows where it will be maintained. This preserves the requirement for explicit agreements.
Study instruction:
- "Review a written action plan for missing owners, vague outputs, unclear dates, or absent follow-up triggers."
Key point:
- An action record should make commitments easy to review and update.
- The written plan should reflect mutual agreement, not just one person's interpretation.

33. Q: What should a manager do when an employee agrees to a commitment but appears uncertain?
A: Show answer
Do not treat a quick 'yes' as proof of shared understanding or genuine commitment. Ask the employee to describe the action, timing, boundaries, and likely obstacles in their own words. Invite them to identify whether the plan is feasible and what support they need. If the proposed commitment is unrealistic, negotiate it rather than allowing an unspoken reservation to remain. The warning against assumptions applies to apparent agreement as well as vague instructions: the parties need an explicit, workable plan.
Study instruction:
- "Use a teach-back question to confirm that both parties understand the commitment in the same way."
Key point:
- A quick acceptance may conceal misunderstanding or reservations about feasibility.
- Inviting clarification before closing makes the commitment more reliable.

34. Q: How does the Jada and Anthony report example illustrate incomplete planning?
A: Show answer
Jada asks Anthony whether he will complete the report, and Anthony enthusiastically agrees, but they do not establish the precise deadline. A week later, Jada expects the report immediately while Anthony believes it is scheduled for the following week. Their conflict arises from different assumptions rather than a clearly negotiated commitment. A complete plan would identify Anthony as the owner, define the report required, state the exact date and time, and establish a progress check if the deadline is important. A promise alone is not a complete agreement.
Study instruction:
- "Rewrite the report agreement using all four WWWF elements and include a progress checkpoint if warranted."
Key point:
- Agreement to complete a task does not establish its deadline.
- The missing timing agreement created the conflict between Jada and Anthony.

35. Q: What does the Adam and Levi creativity example teach about boundaries?
A: Show answer
Adam asks Levi to use more creativity, but the instruction does not define the kind of initiative expected or the limits around major changes. Levi responds by changing the company's inventory system without notifying Adam, while Adam regards the action as unacceptable. The problem is not simply that Levi failed to act; it is that the expectation was open to interpretation. Adam could have described the desired behaviors, such as bringing product ideas and improvement suggestions to weekly meetings, while requiring consultation before significant system changes.
Study instruction:
- "For a delegation involving initiative, define acceptable experiments and decisions requiring prior approval."
Key point:
- Broad behavioral expectations can produce unintended interpretations.
- Examples and decision boundaries clarify how initiative should be exercised.

36. Q: What does the product management and research support example demonstrate about moving to action?
A: Show answer
It demonstrates the importance of staying in the conversation long enough to turn agreement about a problem into specific commitments. The product management team had raised a capability concern because the research group lacked statistical expertise, and a priority concern because some commitments received less attention than others. Just as the groups were preparing to leave, a product manager asked to clarify next steps. That question exposed decisions about staffing, roles, outsourcing, and training that still needed to be made. Without action planning, the discussion could have ended with goodwill but no operational change.
Study instruction:
- "Identify which unresolved decisions would have remained hidden without an explicit next-step discussion."
Key point:
- A diagnosis must be translated into operational choices and assigned actions.
- A next-step question can reveal decisions not visible during the initial discussion.

37. Q: How should an organization turn an identified capability gap into action?
A: Show answer
First specify the capability that is missing and the work that requires it. Then determine who will address the gap and what action will be taken, such as training, role adjustment, staffing change, or obtaining specialist support. Set a date for the action and a follow-up point to verify that the capability has become available and is being used. The chapter's research-team example raises possibilities including additional training, changed roles, staffing adjustments, and outsourcing. The appropriate response depends on the actual work and available options; the key is to make the response explicit and reviewable.
Study instruction:
- "For a real skill gap, compare feasible interventions and define how their effectiveness will be checked."
Key point:
- Capability concerns need a specific intervention, owner, and date.
- Follow-up should verify improved capability in practice, not merely completion of training.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
- Crucial Learning: Crucial Conversations for Accountability
38. Q: How should an organization turn a priority or motivation concern into action?
A: Show answer
Translate the concern into specific commitments about which work receives attention, how competing priorities will be handled, and who will resolve conflicts. In the chapter's example, the research team reliably met deadlines for new product launches but not for legacy products, suggesting that priorities were not aligned. A useful plan would identify the relevant commitments, clarify how work will be prioritized, assign decision ownership when demands conflict, and set a follow-up to review performance across both categories. The plan should address expectations and work priorities, not rely solely on a general request to care more.
Study instruction:
- "Create an explicit priority rule and a review measure for two competing categories of work."
Key point:
- Priority concerns need visible decisions about competing demands.
- Review actual performance across the affected work rather than relying on stated intentions.
Source links:
- McGraw Hill: Crucial Accountability, Third Edition
- Crucial Learning: Crucial Conversations for Accountability
39. Q: How can follow-up test whether an agreed solution has actually worked?
A: Show answer
Check evidence of the intended behavior or result, not merely whether the person completed an activity. If training was agreed, verify that the person can perform the required work. If priorities were to change, review whether commitments across the affected work categories are being met. If a report was promised, confirm that it is complete and meets the agreed requirements. A meeting held or a course attended is not sufficient evidence if the underlying performance issue remains. Follow-up should test whether the action closed the original gap.
Study instruction:
- "For each action, define an outcome measure that demonstrates whether the original gap has narrowed."
Key point:
- Activity completion is not proof that the performance gap has closed.
- Measure the agreed result or behavior against the original expectation.

40. Q: What final questions should participants ask before ending an accountability conversation?
A: Show answer
They should confirm who owns each action, what exactly that person will do or deliver, by what date and time it must be done, and how progress will be checked. They should also clarify material boundaries, quality expectations, dependencies, and what to do if a milestone is blocked or missed. Each participant should know what they must do and what evidence will show progress or completion. The plan is complete when both parties can describe the same next steps and follow-up arrangement without relying on assumptions.
Study instruction:
- "Use this closing checklist in a role-play and identify ambiguity before declaring the plan complete."
Key point:
- A final review catches missing owners, deliverables, deadlines, and follow-up.
- Both parties should be able to restate the same agreement in practical terms.
